## modern-en · philoctetes · 1:11

From this expedition we can infer the character of earlier ones. Mycenæ may have been a small place, and many of the towns of that age may look comparatively insignificant today, but no careful observer would on that account dismiss the estimate given by the poets and by tradition of the size of the force. Suppose Lacedaemon were to be left desolate, with only the temples and the foundations of public buildings standing — as time went on, posterity would be strongly tempted to dismiss her fame as a true measure of her power. Yet they occupy two-fifths of the Peloponnese and lead the whole, to say nothing of their many allies abroad. Still, since the city is neither built in a compact form nor adorned with magnificent temples and public buildings, but is composed of villages in the old style of Hellas, the impression would be one of inadequacy. Whereas if Athens were to suffer the same fate, I suppose any inference from the appearance left behind would make her power seem twice what it really is. We therefore have no right to be sceptical, nor to content ourselves with inspecting a town and ignoring the question of its real power. We may safely conclude that the force in question outdid every earlier one, as it fell short of modern efforts — if we can also accept Homer's testimony here, in which, even allowing for the exaggeration a poet feels licensed to use, we can see that it came nowhere near our own. He puts it at twelve hundred ships, with each Boeotian ship carrying a hundred and twenty men, and each of Philoctetes's ships carrying fifty. By this, I take it, he meant to give the maximum and minimum complements; at any rate, he does not state the size of any others in his catalogue of ships. That all the men were rowers as well as fighters is shown by his account of the ships of Philoctetes, in which all the men at the oars are bowmen. Now it is unlikely that many supernumeraries sailed, except for the kings and senior officers, especially since they had to cross open sea with their war supplies in ships that had no decks but were rigged in the old piratical style. So if we take the average of the largest and smallest ships, the number of men who sailed will look quite small, considering that they represented the whole strength of Hellas. And this was due not so much to a shortage of men as to a shortage of money. The difficulty of provisioning forced the invaders to keep the army down to a size that could live off the country while the war was being fought. Even after the victory they won on landing — and there must have been a victory, or the fortifications of the naval camp could never have been built — there is no sign that they used their full force. On the contrary, they seem to have turned to cultivating the Chersonese and to piracy from lack of supplies. That was what really enabled the Trojans to hold the field against them for ten years: the enemy was always dispersed, so that the Trojans were always a match for the detachment left behind. If the Greeks had brought plenty of supplies with them and had pressed the war without scattering for piracy and farming, they would easily have beaten the Trojans in pitched battle, since they were able to hold their own even with the divided force on duty. In short, if they had stuck to the siege, the capture of Troy would have cost them less time and less trouble. But as lack of money exposed the weakness of earlier expeditions, so for the same reason even this one, more famous than its predecessors, may be judged from what it actually achieved to have fallen short of its renown and of the prevailing opinion of it that has been formed under the influence of the poets.

Whose ships in Homer’s catalogue carry fifty men each, all of them rowers and bowmen — the minimum against the Boeotian hundred and twenty.

## modern-en · ameinocles · 1:13

But as the power of Hellas grew, and the pursuit of wealth became more pressing, the revenues of the states increased, and tyrannies were established almost everywhere on the strength of those revenues — the older form of government having been hereditary monarchy with defined prerogatives. Hellas began to fit out fleets and apply herself more closely to the sea. The Corinthians are said to have been the first to approach the modern style of naval architecture, and Corinth the first place in Hellas where galleys were built. We have Ameinocles, a Corinthian shipwright, building four ships for the Samians. Counting from the end of this present war, it is nearly three hundred years since Ameinocles went to Samos. Likewise, the earliest sea-fight in history was between the Corinthians and Corcyraeans, about two hundred and sixty years ago, dating from the same point. Set on an isthmus, Corinth had from time immemorial been a commercial hub. In the old days nearly all communication between the Hellenes inside and outside the Peloponnese was carried on overland, and Corinthian territory was the highway it travelled. She therefore had great financial resources — as the epithet “wealthy” given to her by the old poets shows — and this enabled her, when sea trade became more common, to acquire her navy and put down piracy. Since she could provide a market for both branches of the trade, she gained for herself all the power that comes from a large revenue. After this, the Ionians built up great naval strength under Cyrus, the first king of the Persians, and his son Cambyses; and during their war with Cyrus, they commanded for a time the Ionian sea. Polycrates also, the tyrant of Samos, had a powerful navy in the reign of Cambyses, with which he reduced many of the islands, including Rhenea, which he consecrated to the Delian Apollo. Around the same time the Phocaeans, while founding Marseilles, defeated the Carthaginians in a sea-fight. These were the most powerful navies. And even these, although so many generations had passed since the Trojan War, seem to have been made up mainly of the old fifty-oars and long-boats, with few galleys among them. It was only shortly before the Persian War, and the death of Darius the successor of Cambyses, that the Sicilian tyrants and the Corcyraeans acquired any large number of galleys. After these, there were no navies of any consequence in Hellas until the expedition of Xerxes. Aegina, Athens, and others may have had a few vessels, but they were mostly fifty-oars. It was right at the end of this period that the war with Aegina and the prospect of the barbarian invasion enabled Themistocles to persuade the Athenians to build the fleet they fought with at Salamis — and even those vessels did not yet have full decks.

The Corinthian shipwright who built four ships for the Samians, nearly three hundred years before the end of this war — Thucydides’s earliest date.

## modern-en · croesus · 1:15

There were various obstacles, too, to national growth in different localities. The power of the Ionians was advancing rapidly when it collided with Persia, under King Cyrus, who, after dethroning Croesus and overrunning everything between the Halys and the sea, did not stop until he had subdued the cities of the coast — leaving the islands to be reduced later by Darius and the Phoenician navy.

## modern-en · hipparchus · 1:19

Now that I have given the results of my inquiries into early times, I grant that some particulars will be hard to believe. The way most men deal with traditions, even the traditions of their own country, is to take them all uncritically as they are handed down. The general Athenian public, for instance, imagines that Hipparchus was tyrant when he was killed by Harmodius and Aristogiton, not knowing that it was Hippias, the eldest of the sons of Pisistratus, who actually held supreme power, and that Hipparchus and Thessalus were his brothers. They do not know that on the very day — at the very moment fixed for the deed — Harmodius and Aristogiton suspected that information had been carried to Hippias by their accomplices, and concluded that he had been warned. They did not attack him, yet, unwilling to be arrested and lose their lives for nothing, they fell upon Hipparchus near the temple of the daughters of Leos and killed him as he was arranging the Panathenaic procession.

Hippias’s brother, killed by Harmodius and Aristogiton near the temple of the daughters of Leos while he was arranging the Panathenaic procession. The Athenian public imagines he was tyrant when he died; Thucydides puts the correction in his chapter on how carelessly men take the traditions even of their own country.

The general Athenian public, for instance, imagines that Hipparchus was tyrant when he was killed by Harmodius and Aristogiton, not knowing that it was Hippias, the eldest of the sons of Pisistratus, who actually held supreme power, and that Hipparchus and Thessalus were his brothers. They do not know that on the very day — at the very moment fixed for the deed — Harmodius and Aristogiton suspected that information had been carried to Hippias by their accomplices, and concluded that he had been warned. They did not attack him, yet, unwilling to be arrested and lose their lives for nothing, they fell upon Hipparchus near the temple of the daughters of Leos and killed him as he was arranging the Panathenaic procession.

Whose sons were Hippias, Hipparchus and Thessalus.

## modern-en · thessalus · 1:19

Now that I have given the results of my inquiries into early times, I grant that some particulars will be hard to believe. The way most men deal with traditions, even the traditions of their own country, is to take them all uncritically as they are handed down. The general Athenian public, for instance, imagines that Hipparchus was tyrant when he was killed by Harmodius and Aristogiton, not knowing that it was Hippias, the eldest of the sons of Pisistratus, who actually held supreme power, and that Hipparchus and Thessalus were his brothers. They do not know that on the very day — at the very moment fixed for the deed — Harmodius and Aristogiton suspected that information had been carried to Hippias by their accomplices, and concluded that he had been warned. They did not attack him, yet, unwilling to be arrested and lose their lives for nothing, they fell upon Hipparchus near the temple of the daughters of Leos and killed him as he was arranging the Panathenaic procession.

The third of Pisistratus’s sons.

## modern-en · phalius · 2:0

The city of Epidamnus stands on the right at the entrance to the Ionic Gulf. The country around it is inhabited by the Taulantians, an Illyrian people. The city is a colony of Corcyra, founded by Phalius, son of Eratocleides, of the Heraclid family. As ancient custom required, he had been summoned for the purpose from Corinth, the mother country. The colonists were joined by some Corinthians and other Dorians. As time went on, Epidamnus grew large and populous; but it fell prey to internal factions, said to have arisen from a war with its barbarian neighbours, and was greatly weakened, losing much of its power. The final act before the war was the people’s expulsion of the nobles. The exiles joined the barbarians and began plundering the city by sea and land. Hard-pressed, the Epidamnians sent ambassadors to Corcyra, begging the mother country not to let them perish, but to settle matters between them and the exiles, and to free them from the war with the barbarians. The ambassadors took their seats as suppliants in the temple of Hera and made these requests of the Corcyraeans. But the Corcyraeans refused their supplication and dismissed them empty-handed.

Eratocleides’s son, of the Heraclid family, summoned from Corinth as ancient custom required to found Epidamnus for Corcyra.

## modern-en · eratocleides · 2:0

The city of Epidamnus stands on the right at the entrance to the Ionic Gulf. The country around it is inhabited by the Taulantians, an Illyrian people. The city is a colony of Corcyra, founded by Phalius, son of Eratocleides, of the Heraclid family. As ancient custom required, he had been summoned for the purpose from Corinth, the mother country. The colonists were joined by some Corinthians and other Dorians. As time went on, Epidamnus grew large and populous; but it fell prey to internal factions, said to have arisen from a war with its barbarian neighbours, and was greatly weakened, losing much of its power. The final act before the war was the people’s expulsion of the nobles. The exiles joined the barbarians and began plundering the city by sea and land. Hard-pressed, the Epidamnians sent ambassadors to Corcyra, begging the mother country not to let them perish, but to settle matters between them and the exiles, and to free them from the war with the barbarians. The ambassadors took their seats as suppliants in the temple of Hera and made these requests of the Corcyraeans. But the Corcyraeans refused their supplication and dismissed them empty-handed.

Phalius’s father.

## modern-en · aristeus-pellichas · 2:4

Turning a deaf ear to all these proposals, the Corinthians—their ships manned and their allies arrived—sent a herald ahead to declare war and put to sea with seventy-five ships and two thousand heavy infantry, sailing for Epidamnus to give battle to the Corcyraeans. The fleet was commanded by Aristeus, son of Pellichas, Callicrates, son of Callias, and Timanor, son of Timanthes; the troops by Archetimus, son of Eurytimus, and Isarchidas, son of Isarchus. When they reached Actium, in the territory of Anactorium, at the mouth of the Gulf of Ambracia where the temple of Apollo stands, the Corcyraeans sent ahead a herald in a light boat to warn them not to sail against them. Meanwhile the Corcyraeans manned their ships, all of them equipped for action, the older vessels girded with cables to keep them seaworthy. When the herald returned without any peaceful answer from the Corinthians, their ships now manned, they put out to sea to meet the enemy with a fleet of eighty sail (the other forty were engaged in the siege of Epidamnus), formed line, and went into battle. They won decisively, destroying fifteen Corinthian vessels. That same day Epidamnus was forced by its besiegers to capitulate; the terms were that the foreigners be sold and the Corinthians held as prisoners of war until their fate could be otherwise decided.

Pellichas’s son, first-named of the three admirals of the seventy-five ships Corinth sent to Epidamnus — and beaten off Leukimme with fifteen ships lost. Not the Aristeus who commanded at Potidaea.

## modern-en · meikiades · 2:21

Meanwhile the Corinthians completed their preparations and sailed for Corcyra with one hundred and fifty ships. Of these Elis furnished ten, Megara twelve, Leucas ten, Ambracia twenty-seven, Anactorium one, and Corinth herself ninety. Each contingent had its own admiral, the Corinthian commander being Xenoclides, son of Euthycles, with four colleagues. Sailing from Leucas, they made land on the part of the continent opposite Corcyra. They anchored in the harbour of Chimerium, in Thesprotis. Above it, at some distance from the sea, lies the city of Ephyre, in the Elean district. Near this city the Acherusian lake pours its waters into the sea. The lake gets its name from the river Acheron, which flows through Thesprotis and falls into it. The river Thyamis also flows there, forming the boundary between Thesprotis and Kestrine; between these rivers rises the point of Chimerium. In this part of the continent the Corinthians now anchored and formed an encampment. When the Corcyraeans saw them coming, they manned one hundred and ten ships, commanded by Meikiades, Aisimides, and Eurybatus, and stationed themselves at one of the Sybota islands; the ten Athenian ships were present. On Point Leukimme they posted their land forces and one thousand heavy infantry who had come from Zacynthus to help. The Corinthians on the mainland had allies of their own. The barbarians flocked in large numbers to their assistance, the inhabitants of this part of the continent being old allies of theirs.

First-named of the three admirals of the hundred and ten Corcyraean ships at Sybota, each commanding one squadron of the line.

## modern-en · lacedaemonius · 2:20

When the Athenians had heard both sides, two assemblies were held. In the first, public feeling was clearly inclined to listen to Corinth. In the second, the mood had shifted, and an alliance with Corcyra was decided on, with certain reservations. It was to be a defensive, not an offensive alliance. It would not violate the treaty with the Peloponnese: Athens could not be required to join Corcyra in any attack on Corinth. But each of the contracting parties had a right to the other’s help against invasion, whether of its own territory or of an ally’s. It was now beginning to be felt that the coming of the Peloponnesian war was only a matter of time, and no one wanted to see a naval power as great as Corcyra sacrificed to Corinth. Yet if the two could be left to weaken each other by mutual conflict, that would be useful preparation for the struggle Athens might one day have to wage with Corinth and the other naval powers. At the same time the island lay conveniently on the coastal passage to Italy and Sicily. With these views Athens received Corcyra into alliance and, soon after the Corinthians had departed, sent ten ships to her assistance. They were commanded by Lacedaemonius, son of Cimon, Diotimus, son of Strombichus, and Proteas, son of Epicles. Their instructions were to avoid collision with the Corinthian fleet except under certain circumstances. If it sailed to Corcyra and threatened a landing on her coast or any of her possessions, they were to do their utmost to prevent it. These instructions came from a wish to avoid breaking the treaty.

Cimon’s son, first-named of the three commanders of the ten Athenian ships sent to Corcyra under orders not to collide with the Corinthian fleet — orders they kept until the rout made them impossible.

## modern-en · cimon · 2:20

Yet if the two could be left to weaken each other by mutual conflict, that would be useful preparation for the struggle Athens might one day have to wage with Corinth and the other naval powers. With these views Athens received Corcyra into alliance and, soon after the Corinthians had departed, sent ten ships to her assistance. If it sailed to Corcyra and threatened a landing on her coast or any of her possessions, they were to do their utmost to prevent it. These instructions came from a wish to avoid breaking the treaty.

Miltiades’s son. He took Eion on the Strymon from the Medes and enslaved it, enslaved Scyros, won both battles at the Eurymedon on the same day and destroyed two hundred Phoenician ships, brought the Athenians to Ithome when Sparta asked for their siege-craft, and died at the siege of Kitium in Cyprus. Lacedaemonius was his son.

## modern-en · miltiades · 4:6

First the Athenians besieged and captured Eion on the Strymon from the Medes, and enslaved the inhabitants, under the command of Cimon, son of Miltiades. Next they enslaved Scyros, the island in the Aegean, which had a Dolopian population, and colonized it themselves. Then came a war against Carystus, in which the rest of Euboea remained neutral; it ended in surrender on terms. After this Naxos left the confederacy, war followed, and after a siege she was forced back in. This was the first instance of the agreement being broken by the subjugation of an allied city — a precedent the rest followed in the order circumstances dictated. Of all the causes of defection, the chief was failure to keep up the tribute and the supply of vessels, and failure to perform service. The Athenians were severe and exacting, and they made themselves offensive by applying the screw to men who were not used to and not really inclined to any continuous effort. In other respects too the Athenians were no longer the popular leaders they had been at the start. If they took more than their fair share of the work, it was correspondingly easy for them to bring back into line any state that tried to leave the confederacy. The allies had themselves to blame for this. Most of them, wanting to avoid service, arranged to pay their share of the cost in money instead of in ships, so as not to leave home. So while Athens was building up her navy on the funds they contributed, a revolt would always find them without resources or experience for war.

Cimon’s father.

## modern-en · diotimus · 2:20

When the Athenians had heard both sides, two assemblies were held. In the first, public feeling was clearly inclined to listen to Corinth. In the second, the mood had shifted, and an alliance with Corcyra was decided on, with certain reservations. It was to be a defensive, not an offensive alliance. It would not violate the treaty with the Peloponnese: Athens could not be required to join Corcyra in any attack on Corinth. But each of the contracting parties had a right to the other’s help against invasion, whether of its own territory or of an ally’s. It was now beginning to be felt that the coming of the Peloponnesian war was only a matter of time, and no one wanted to see a naval power as great as Corcyra sacrificed to Corinth. Yet if the two could be left to weaken each other by mutual conflict, that would be useful preparation for the struggle Athens might one day have to wage with Corinth and the other naval powers. At the same time the island lay conveniently on the coastal passage to Italy and Sicily. With these views Athens received Corcyra into alliance and, soon after the Corinthians had departed, sent ten ships to her assistance. They were commanded by Lacedaemonius, son of Cimon, Diotimus, son of Strombichus, and Proteas, son of Epicles. Their instructions were to avoid collision with the Corinthian fleet except under certain circumstances. If it sailed to Corcyra and threatened a landing on her coast or any of her possessions, they were to do their utmost to prevent it. These instructions came from a wish to avoid breaking the treaty.

Strombichus’s son, the second of the three commanders of the ten ships.

## modern-en · glaucon · 2:23

After the rout the Corinthians, instead of lashing fast and towing off the hulls of the vessels they had disabled, turned their attention to the men, butchering them as they sailed through, more interested in killing than in taking prisoners. They even slew some of their own friends by mistake, not knowing that the right wing had been defeated. The number of ships on both sides, and the distance over which they covered the sea, made it hard, once the lines had joined, to tell the conquering ships from the conquered. This battle was far greater than any before it, at least between Hellenes, in the number of vessels engaged. After they had chased the Corcyraeans to land, the Corinthians turned to the wrecks and their dead, most of whom they managed to recover and convey to Sybota, the rendezvous of the land forces furnished by their barbarian allies. Sybota, it should be noted, is a deserted harbour in Thesprotis. This task done, they re-formed and sailed against the Corcyraeans, who advanced to meet them with all the ships still fit for service, accompanied by the Athenian vessels, fearing that they might attempt a landing on their territory. It was getting late, and the paean had been sung for the attack, when the Corinthians suddenly began to back water. They had spotted twenty Athenian ships sailing up. These had been sent out afterwards to reinforce the original ten by the Athenians, who feared—as it turned out, justly—the defeat of the Corcyraeans and the inability of so few ships to protect them. The Corinthians saw these ships first. They suspected they were from Athens, and that what they saw was not all but that more were behind, so they began to retire. The Corcyraeans had not yet sighted them, since they were advancing from a point harder for them to see, and were wondering why the Corinthians were backing water, when some of them caught sight of the ships and cried out that there were sails in view ahead. At that they too withdrew, for it was now getting dark, and the retreat of the Corinthians had ended hostilities. So the two sides parted, and the battle ceased with night. The Corcyraeans were in their camp at Leukimme when these twenty Athenian ships, under the command of Glaucon, son of Leagrus, and Andocides, son of Leogoras, made their way through the corpses and the wrecks and sailed up to the camp not long after they were sighted. It was now night, and the Corcyraeans feared they might be hostile vessels; but they soon recognised them, and the ships came to anchor.

Leagrus’s son, who brought the twenty Athenian reinforcing ships up through the corpses and the wrecks to the Corcyraean camp at Leukimme after dark — and was very nearly taken for the enemy.

## modern-en · perdiccas · 2:28

Almost immediately after this, fresh disputes arose between the Athenians and the Peloponnesians and contributed their share to the war. Corinth was making schemes for revenge, and Athens suspected her hostility. The Potidæans, who inhabit the isthmus of Pallene, being a Corinthian colony but tributary allies of Athens, were ordered to raze the wall facing Pallene, give hostages, dismiss the Corinthian magistrates, and refuse in future to receive the men sent annually from Corinth to succeed them. It was feared that they might be persuaded by Perdiccas and the Corinthians to revolt, and might draw the rest of the allies in the direction of Thrace into revolt with them. These precautions against the Potidæans were taken by the Athenians immediately after the battle at Corcyra. Not only was Corinth at last openly hostile, but Perdiccas, son of Alexander, king of the Macedonians, had been turned from an old friend and ally into an enemy. He had become an enemy because the Athenians had entered into alliance with his brother Philip and with Derdas, who were leagued against him. In his alarm he had sent to Lacedaemon to try to involve the Athenians in war with the Peloponnesians, and was trying to win over Corinth in order to bring about the revolt of Potidæa. He also made overtures to the Chalcidians toward Thrace, and to the Bottiaeans, urging them to join in the revolt, for he thought that if these places on the border could be made his allies, the war would be easier to wage with their cooperation. Aware of all this and wanting to forestall the revolt of the cities, the Athenians acted as follows. They were just then sending off thirty ships and a thousand heavy infantry for his country under the command of Archestratus, son of Lycomedes, with four colleagues. They instructed the captains to take hostages from the Potidæans, raze the wall, and be on guard against the revolt of the neighbouring cities.

Alexander’s son and king of the Macedonians, turned from an old Athenian friend and ally into an enemy because Athens allied with his brother Philip and with Derdas. He worked on Lacedaemon and on Corinth to get Potidaea into revolt, persuaded the Chalcidians to demolish their seaboard towns and settle inland at Olynthus on land he gave them round Lake Bolbe, and changed sides twice in one campaign — deputing Iolaus to command the cavalry he had just brought back to the Potidaean side.

## modern-en · pausanias-macedon · 2:31

The Athenians too immediately received news of the revolt of the cities. On learning that Aristeus and his reinforcements were on their way, they sent two thousand heavy infantry of their own citizens and forty ships against the rebellious places, under the command of Callias, son of Calliades, with four colleagues. They reached Macedonia first and found the force of a thousand men sent out earlier just become masters of Therme and besieging Pydna. They joined the siege and besieged Pydna for a time. They later came to terms and concluded a forced alliance with Perdiccas, hastened by the calls of Potidæa and by the arrival of Aristeus there. They withdrew from Macedonia, going to Beroea and from there to Strepsa. After a futile attempt on Strepsa, they pressed on by land to Potidæa with three thousand heavy infantry of their own citizens, besides a number of allies and six hundred Macedonian horsemen, the followers of Philip and Pausanias. With them sailed seventy ships along the coast. Advancing by short marches, on the third day they reached Gigonus and encamped there.

The Macedonian whose horsemen rode with Philip’s on the Athenian side to Potidaea. Thucydides says nothing else about him, and he is not the Spartan regent.

## modern-en · aristeus-adimantus · 2:30

Meanwhile the Corinthians, with Potidæa in revolt and the Athenian ships on the coast of Macedonia, alarmed for the safety of the place and feeling its danger as their own, sent volunteers from Corinth and mercenaries from the rest of the Peloponnese—sixteen hundred heavy infantry in all, and four hundred light troops. Aristeus, son of Adimantus, who was always a steady friend of the Potidæans, took command of the expedition; it was largely out of love for him that most of the men from Corinth volunteered. They reached Thrace forty days after the revolt of Potidæa.

Adimantus’s son, always a steady friend of the Potidaeans — most of the Corinthian volunteers went out of love for him. Made general of all the infantry, he won with his own wing and lost with the rest, and got most of his men into Potidaea at a run along the breakwater through the sea. When the investment closed he advised all but five hundred to sail out so the provisions would last, offered to stay himself, could not persuade them, and slipped past the guardships alone to carry on the war from Chalcidice. Not the admiral beaten at Epidamnus.

## modern-en · adimantus · 2:30

Meanwhile the Corinthians, with Potidæa in revolt and the Athenian ships on the coast of Macedonia, alarmed for the safety of the place and feeling its danger as their own, sent volunteers from Corinth and mercenaries from the rest of the Peloponnese—sixteen hundred heavy infantry in all, and four hundred light troops. Aristeus, son of Adimantus, who was always a steady friend of the Potidæans, took command of the expedition; it was largely out of love for him that most of the men from Corinth volunteered. They reached Thrace forty days after the revolt of Potidæa.

Aristeus of Potidaea’s father.

## modern-en · callias-calliades · 2:31

The Athenians too immediately received news of the revolt of the cities. On learning that Aristeus and his reinforcements were on their way, they sent two thousand heavy infantry of their own citizens and forty ships against the rebellious places, under the command of Callias, son of Calliades, with four colleagues. They reached Macedonia first and found the force of a thousand men sent out earlier just become masters of Therme and besieging Pydna. They joined the siege and besieged Pydna for a time. They later came to terms and concluded a forced alliance with Perdiccas, hastened by the calls of Potidæa and by the arrival of Aristeus there. They withdrew from Macedonia, going to Beroea and from there to Strepsa. After a futile attempt on Strepsa, they pressed on by land to Potidæa with three thousand heavy infantry of their own citizens, besides a number of allies and six hundred Macedonian horsemen, the followers of Philip and Pausanias. With them sailed seventy ships along the coast. Advancing by short marches, on the third day they reached Gigonus and encamped there.

Calliades’s son, the Athenian general of the two thousand infantry and forty ships sent against the revolted towns, and one of the hundred and fifty Athenian dead in the victory at Potidaea. Not the Corinthian admiral’s father.

## modern-en · iolaus · 2:32

Meanwhile the Potidæans and the Peloponnesians under Aristeus were encamped on the side facing Olynthus, on the isthmus, awaiting the Athenians, with their market set up outside the city. The allies had made Aristeus general of all the infantry, while command of the cavalry was given to Perdiccas, who had at once left the Athenian alliance and gone back to that of the Potidæans, deputing Iolaus as his general. The plan of Aristeus was to keep his own force on the isthmus and await the Athenian attack, leaving the Chalcidians and the allies outside the isthmus, and the two hundred cavalry from Perdiccas in Olynthus, to fall on the Athenian rear when they advanced against him—putting the enemy between two fires. Callias, the Athenian general, and his colleagues sent the Macedonian horse and a few of the allies to Olynthus to prevent any movement from that quarter. The Athenians themselves broke up their camp and marched against Potidæa. When they reached the isthmus and saw the enemy preparing for battle, they formed against him, and the engagement followed soon after. The wing of Aristeus, with the Corinthians and other picked troops around him, routed the wing opposed to it and pursued for a considerable distance. But the rest of the Potidæan and Peloponnesian army was defeated by the Athenians and took refuge inside the fortifications. Returning from the pursuit, Aristeus saw the defeat of the rest of the army. At a loss which of the two risks to take—going to Olynthus or to Potidæa—he finally decided to draw his men into as small a space as possible and force his way at a run into Potidæa. Not without difficulty, through a storm of missiles, he passed along the breakwater through the sea, and brought off most of his men safe, though a few were lost. Meanwhile the auxiliaries of the Potidæans from Olynthus, which is about seven miles off and in sight of Potidæa, when the battle began and the signals went up, advanced a little way to help; the Macedonian horse formed against them to prevent it. But when victory quickly declared for the Athenians and the signals were taken down, they retired back behind the wall, and the Macedonians returned to the Athenians. So no cavalry was present on either side. After the battle the Athenians set up a trophy and gave back the dead to the Potidæans under truce. The Potidæans and their allies had close to three hundred killed; the Athenians, one hundred and fifty of their own citizens, including their general, Callias.

Perdiccas’s deputy in command of the cavalry at Potidaea.

## modern-en · phormio · 2:33

Siege-works were now raised and manned by the Athenians on the isthmus side of the wall. On the Pallene side none were raised. They did not feel strong enough at once to keep a garrison on the isthmus and cross over to Pallene to raise works there; they feared that the Potidæans and their allies might take advantage of their division to attack them. Meanwhile the Athenians at home, learning that there were no works on the Pallene side, some time later sent out sixteen hundred heavy infantry of their own citizens under the command of Phormio, son of Asopius. On reaching Pallene he fixed his headquarters at Aphytis and led his army against Potidæa by short marches, ravaging the country as he advanced. Since no one ventured to meet him in the field, he raised works against the wall on the Pallene side. So at last Potidæa was strongly invested on both sides, and from the sea by the ships cooperating in the blockade. Aristeus, seeing the investment complete and having no hope of saving the place except in the event of some movement from the Peloponnese, or some other improbable contingency, advised that all but five hundred men should watch for a wind and sail out of the place, so that provisions might last longer. He was willing to remain himself among those who stayed. Unable to persuade them, and wishing to take the next best course and have things outside in the best possible state, he eluded the Athenian guardships and sailed out. Remaining with the Chalcidians, he carried on the war: in particular he laid an ambush near the city of the Sermylians and cut off many of them; he also communicated with the Peloponnese and tried to contrive some method of bringing help. Meanwhile, after the investment of Potidæa was complete, Phormio used his sixteen hundred men to ravage Chalcidice and Bottica, and took some of the towns.

Asopius’s son, who brought sixteen hundred Athenian infantry round to the Pallene side of Potidaea, fixed his headquarters at Aphytis, and raised the works nobody had dared to raise — closing the investment on both sides. He then used the same force to ravage Chalcidice and Bottica and take some of the towns.

## modern-en · archidamus · 3:14

Such were the words of the Athenians. After the Lacedaemonians had heard the complaints of the allies against the Athenians, and the response of the Athenians, they made everyone withdraw and consulted privately on the question before them. The opinions of the majority all pointed to the same conclusion: the Athenians were open aggressors, and war must be declared at once. But Archidamus, the Lacedaemonian king, who had the reputation of being both wise and moderate, came forward and made the following speech:

The Lacedaemonian king with the reputation of being wise and moderate, who told the assembly what a war against a distant people with money, ships, horses and tributary allies would cost: they were inferior at sea, far worse off for money, and could not end it by burning fields. He asked for embassies and two or three years of preparation, said he feared they would leave the war as a legacy to their children, and was voted down.

## modern-en · sthenelaidas · 3:19

Such were the words of Archidamus. Last came forward Sthenelaidas, one of the ephors for that year, who spoke to the Lacedaemonians as follows:

The ephor who answered Archidamus — the Athenians had said a great deal in praise of themselves and nowhere denied the wrong, so they deserved double punishment, for having ceased to be good and for having become bad. He then refused to judge the shouting and made the assembly divide by walking to two sides of the room, so that they would have to declare themselves and their ardour for war would rise.

## modern-en · leotychides · 4:0

Here is how Athens came to find herself in the position from which her power grew. After the Medes had been beaten by sea and land by the Hellenes and had retreated from Europe, and after those who had fled with their ships to Mycale had been wiped out, Leotychides, king of the Lacedaemonians and commander of the Hellenes at Mycale, went home with the allies from the Peloponnese. The Athenians and the allies from Ionia and the Hellespont, who had now revolted from the King, stayed behind and laid siege to Sestos, which the Medes still held. They wintered before it, and when the barbarians evacuated the place they took it; after that they sailed back from the Hellespont to their own cities. Meanwhile, with the barbarian gone from their country, the Athenian people at once began bringing back their children, their wives, and what property they had left from the places where they had stored them, and prepared to rebuild their city and their walls. Only isolated stretches of the wall were still standing, and most of the houses were in ruins, though a few remained — the ones the Persian grandees had used as quarters.

King of the Lacedaemonians and commander of the Hellenes at Mycale, who went home with the Peloponnesian allies while the Athenians and the Ionians stayed to take Sestos.

## modern-en · themistocles · 1:13

But as the power of Hellas grew, and the pursuit of wealth became more pressing, the revenues of the states increased, and tyrannies were established almost everywhere on the strength of those revenues — the older form of government having been hereditary monarchy with defined prerogatives. Hellas began to fit out fleets and apply herself more closely to the sea. The Corinthians are said to have been the first to approach the modern style of naval architecture, and Corinth the first place in Hellas where galleys were built. We have Ameinocles, a Corinthian shipwright, building four ships for the Samians. Counting from the end of this present war, it is nearly three hundred years since Ameinocles went to Samos. Likewise, the earliest sea-fight in history was between the Corinthians and Corcyraeans, about two hundred and sixty years ago, dating from the same point. Set on an isthmus, Corinth had from time immemorial been a commercial hub. In the old days nearly all communication between the Hellenes inside and outside the Peloponnese was carried on overland, and Corinthian territory was the highway it travelled. She therefore had great financial resources — as the epithet “wealthy” given to her by the old poets shows — and this enabled her, when sea trade became more common, to acquire her navy and put down piracy. Since she could provide a market for both branches of the trade, she gained for herself all the power that comes from a large revenue. After this, the Ionians built up great naval strength under Cyrus, the first king of the Persians, and his son Cambyses; and during their war with Cyrus, they commanded for a time the Ionian sea. Polycrates also, the tyrant of Samos, had a powerful navy in the reign of Cambyses, with which he reduced many of the islands, including Rhenea, which he consecrated to the Delian Apollo. Around the same time the Phocaeans, while founding Marseilles, defeated the Carthaginians in a sea-fight. These were the most powerful navies. And even these, although so many generations had passed since the Trojan War, seem to have been made up mainly of the old fifty-oars and long-boats, with few galleys among them. It was only shortly before the Persian War, and the death of Darius the successor of Cambyses, that the Sicilian tyrants and the Corcyraeans acquired any large number of galleys. After these, there were no navies of any consequence in Hellas until the expedition of Xerxes. Aegina, Athens, and others may have had a few vessels, but they were mostly fifty-oars. It was right at the end of this period that the war with Aegina and the prospect of the barbarian invasion enabled Themistocles to persuade the Athenians to build the fleet they fought with at Salamis — and even those vessels did not yet have full decks.

The commander at Salamis, whom Thucydides calls the ablest judge of a sudden crisis and the best prophet of a distant future that ever was — genius by native ability, neither shaped nor supplemented by study. He got Athens walled by going to Sparta himself and stalling there while the whole city, women and children included, tore down buildings for materials; he finished Piraeus, told Athens to stick to the sea, and laid the foundations of the empire.

## modern-en · abronichus · 4:1

When the Lacedaemonians realized what the Athenians were planning, they sent an embassy to Athens. They themselves would have preferred not to see Athens or any other city behind walls, but they were acting mainly at the urging of their allies, who were alarmed by the strength of her newly built navy and the courage she had shown in the war against the Medes. They asked the Athenians not only to refrain from building walls of their own, but also to join them in tearing down such walls as still stood in cities outside the Peloponnese. The real point of this advice — the suspicion of Athens behind it — was not openly stated. The line they took was that if the barbarian invaded a third time, he would have no strong base of operations such as Thebes had given him before, and that the Peloponnese would be enough for everyone, both as a refuge and as a launching point. After the Lacedaemonians had said this, the Athenians, on Themistocles’ advice, dismissed them at once, with the answer that envoys would be sent to Sparta to discuss the question. Themistocles told the Athenians to send him off to Lacedaemon as fast as possible, but to delay sending his colleagues, even after they had been chosen, until the wall had been raised to a height from which it could be defended. In the meantime the whole population of the city — the Athenians, their wives, and their children — was to work on the wall, sparing no building, public or private, that could be of any use; everything was to be torn down for materials. After giving these orders and adding that he would handle everything else at Sparta himself, he set off. When he arrived at Lacedaemon he did not seek an audience with the authorities but stalled and made excuses. Whenever any member of the government asked him why he had not appeared in the assembly, he said he was waiting for his colleagues, who had been held up in Athens by some piece of business; he expected them shortly, and was puzzled they were not yet there. At first the Lacedaemonians believed him, out of friendship for him; but as more and more people kept arriving from Athens, all clearly stating that the work was going on and was already getting some height, they could no longer disbelieve it. Aware of this, Themistocles told them that rumours were unreliable and should not be trusted: they should send respectable people from Sparta to inspect, men whose report could be trusted. They duly dispatched them. Themistocles secretly sent word to the Athenians to detain these men as far as possible without putting them under open restraint, and not to let them go until he and his colleagues had returned home. For his colleagues — Abronichus, son of Lysicles, and Aristides, son of Lysimachus — had now joined him, with the news that the wall was high enough; and he was afraid that once the Lacedaemonians heard the truth, they might refuse to release them. So the Athenians detained the envoys as Themistocles had instructed, and Themistocles got his audience with the Lacedaemonians and at last told them outright that Athens was now fortified well enough to protect its inhabitants; that any embassy the Lacedaemonians or their allies wished to send to Athens in future should approach her on the assumption that she could tell what was in her own and the common interest. When the Athenians had decided to abandon their city and take to their ships, they had taken that dangerous step without consulting Sparta; and on every other occasion when they had deliberated together with the Lacedaemonians, their judgement had proved second to none. They now thought it right that their city should have a wall, and that this would be more to the advantage of both the Athenian citizens and the Hellenic confederacy as a whole; for without equal military strength it was impossible to contribute equal or fair counsel to the common cause. Either, he pointed out, all members of the confederacy should be without walls, or what Athens had just done should be accepted as right.

Lysicles’s son, one of the two colleagues Themistocles had kept back in Athens until the wall was high enough to defend.

## modern-en · pausanias-sparta · 4:4

Meanwhile Pausanias, son of Cleombrotus, was sent out from Lacedaemon as commander-in-chief of the Hellenes, with twenty ships from the Peloponnese. He sailed with thirty Athenian ships and a number of other allies. They mounted an expedition against Cyprus and subdued most of the island, and afterwards against Byzantium, which was held by the Medes, and forced it to surrender. This happened while the Spartans were still in command. But Pausanias’ violence had already begun to alienate the Hellenes, particularly the Ionians and the recently liberated populations. They turned to the Athenians and asked them, as kinsmen, to take the lead and put a stop to any violence on Pausanias’ part. The Athenians accepted the offer and resolved both to check him and to settle everything else as their own interests seemed to require. In the meantime the Lacedaemonians recalled Pausanias to investigate the reports they had received. Numerous and serious accusations had been brought against him by Hellenes arriving in Sparta; and to all appearances he had played the role of an autocrat more than that of a general. As it happened, his recall coincided with the moment when the hatred he had aroused had driven the allies, except for the soldiers from the Peloponnese, to desert him and side with the Athenians. On his return to Lacedaemon he was censured for his private acts of oppression but acquitted on the heaviest charges and pronounced not guilty — though it should be noted that the charge of Medism was one of the principal counts and, by all appearances, one of the best founded. The Lacedaemonians did not, however, restore him to his command. They sent out Dorkis and a few others with a small force, who found the allies no longer willing to grant them the supremacy. Seeing this, they left, and the Lacedaemonians did not send anyone to replace them. They feared that those who went out would deteriorate as Pausanias had; besides, they wanted to be done with the Median War, and they were satisfied that the Athenians were up to the position and at that moment friendly toward themselves.

Cleombrotus’s son, sent out as commander-in-chief of the Hellenes, who took Cyprus and Byzantium and then lost the command by playing the autocrat: the Ionians and the newly liberated went over to the Athenians to be rid of him, and Sparta recalled him on charges of which Medism was the best founded and acquitted him of it.

## modern-en · cleombrotus · 4:4

Meanwhile Pausanias, son of Cleombrotus, was sent out from Lacedaemon as commander-in-chief of the Hellenes, with twenty ships from the Peloponnese. He sailed with thirty Athenian ships and a number of other allies. They mounted an expedition against Cyprus and subdued most of the island, and afterwards against Byzantium, which was held by the Medes, and forced it to surrender. This happened while the Spartans were still in command. But Pausanias’ violence had already begun to alienate the Hellenes, particularly the Ionians and the recently liberated populations. They turned to the Athenians and asked them, as kinsmen, to take the lead and put a stop to any violence on Pausanias’ part. The Athenians accepted the offer and resolved both to check him and to settle everything else as their own interests seemed to require. In the meantime the Lacedaemonians recalled Pausanias to investigate the reports they had received. Numerous and serious accusations had been brought against him by Hellenes arriving in Sparta; and to all appearances he had played the role of an autocrat more than that of a general. As it happened, his recall coincided with the moment when the hatred he had aroused had driven the allies, except for the soldiers from the Peloponnese, to desert him and side with the Athenians. On his return to Lacedaemon he was censured for his private acts of oppression but acquitted on the heaviest charges and pronounced not guilty — though it should be noted that the charge of Medism was one of the principal counts and, by all appearances, one of the best founded. The Lacedaemonians did not, however, restore him to his command. They sent out Dorkis and a few others with a small force, who found the allies no longer willing to grant them the supremacy. Seeing this, they left, and the Lacedaemonians did not send anyone to replace them. They feared that those who went out would deteriorate as Pausanias had; besides, they wanted to be done with the Median War, and they were satisfied that the Athenians were up to the position and at that moment friendly toward themselves.

Pausanias the regent’s father, and Nicomedes’s.

## modern-en · hellanicus · 4:5

The Athenians, having thus succeeded to the supremacy by the voluntary act of the allies, who hated Pausanias, decided which cities were to contribute money against the barbarian and which were to contribute ships. Their stated aim was to retaliate for what they had suffered by ravaging the King’s country. It was now that the office of “Treasurers for Hellas” was first established by the Athenians. These officials received the tribute, as the contributed money was called. The tribute was first fixed at four hundred and sixty talents. The common treasury was at Delos, and the congresses were held in the temple. Their supremacy began with allies who were independent and acted on the resolutions of a common congress. It was marked by the following undertakings, in war and in administration, during the interval between the Median War and the present war — against the barbarian, against their own rebellious allies, and against the Peloponnesian powers they came into contact with on various occasions. My reason for relating these events, and for venturing on this digression, is that this stretch of history has been left out by all my predecessors, who confined themselves either to Hellenic history before the Median War or to the Median War itself. Hellanicus did touch on these events in his Athenian history, but he is rather brief and not accurate in his dates. Besides, the history of these events explains the growth of the Athenian empire.

Who touched on the fifty years between the wars in his Athenian history — briefly, and not accurately in his dates. The only predecessor Thucydides names.

## modern-en · inaros · 4:10

Meanwhile Inaros, son of Psammetichus, a Libyan king of the Libyans on the Egyptian border, with his headquarters at Marea, the town above Pharos, caused almost the whole of Egypt to revolt from King Artaxerxes. Putting himself at the head of the revolt, he invited the Athenians to come to his aid. Abandoning a Cyprian expedition on which they happened to be engaged with two hundred ships of their own and their allies, the Athenians arrived in Egypt, sailed up from the sea into the Nile, took control of the river and of two-thirds of Memphis, and then turned to attacking the remaining third, which is called White Castle. Inside it were Persians and Medes who had taken refuge there, and Egyptians who had not joined the revolt.

Psammetichus’s son, a Libyan king of the Libyans on the Egyptian border with his headquarters at Marea, who raised almost all Egypt against Artaxerxes and called in the Athenians — and who was betrayed, captured and crucified.

Xerxes’s son, newly come to the throne when Themistocles wrote to him; the king Egypt revolted from.

## modern-en · amyrtaeus · 4:14

Meanwhile the Athenians and their allies were still in Egypt, going through every kind of fortune in war. At first the Athenians were masters of Egypt, and the King sent Megabazus, a Persian, to Lacedaemon with money to bribe the Peloponnesians to invade Attica and so draw the Athenians off from Egypt. When this made no progress and the money was simply being wasted, he recalled Megabazus with what was left of it and sent Megabuzus, son of Zopyrus, a Persian, with a large army into Egypt. Coming by land, he defeated the Egyptians and their allies in a battle, drove the Hellenes out of Memphis, and finally penned them up on the island of Prosopitis, where he besieged them for a year and a half. At last he drained the canal, diverting the water into another channel, leaving their ships high and dry and joining most of the island to the mainland. He then marched across on foot and took the place. So after six years of war the Hellenic enterprise came to ruin. Of all that great force, a few got through Libya safely to Cyrene, but most perished. Egypt returned to its subjection to the King, except for Amyrtaeus, the king in the marshes, whom they could not capture because of the size of the marsh — the marshmen also being the most warlike of the Egyptians. Inaros, the Libyan king who alone had set off the Egyptian revolt, was betrayed, captured, and crucified. Meanwhile a relieving squadron of fifty ships had set out from Athens and the rest of the confederacy for Egypt. They put in at the Mendesian mouth of the Nile, with no idea of what had happened. Attacked on the land side by the troops, and from the sea by the Phoenician navy, most of the ships were destroyed; the few survivors got away by retreat. Such was the end of the great Athenian and allied expedition to Egypt.

The king in the marshes, whom the Persians could not take because of the size of the marsh and because the marshmen are the most warlike of the Egyptians — and who afterwards got sixty of Cimon’s ships.

## modern-en · leocrates · 4:11

Meanwhile the Athenians, making a landing from their fleet at Haliae, were engaged by a force of Corinthians and Epidaurians, and the Corinthians won. Afterwards the Athenians fought the Peloponnesian fleet off Cecruphalia, and the Athenians won. Then war broke out between Aegina and Athens. There was a great sea battle off Aegina between Athenians and Aeginetans, each side helped by their allies; the Athenians won, taking seventy of the enemy’s ships. They landed and began a siege of the country under the command of Leocrates, son of Stroebus. At this the Peloponnesians, wanting to help the Aeginetans, threw three hundred heavy infantry — who had previously been serving with the Corinthians and Epidaurians — into Aegina. Meanwhile the Corinthians and their allies seized the heights of Geraneia and marched down into the Megarid, in the belief that with so large an Athenian force absent in Aegina and Egypt, Athens would be unable to help the Megarians without lifting the siege of Aegina. But the Athenians, instead of moving the army at Aegina, raised a force from the old men and the boys who had been left in the city, and marched into the Megarid under the command of Myronides. After a drawn battle with the Corinthians, the two sides parted, each thinking they had won. The Athenians had rather the better of it, and after the Corinthians left they set up a trophy. Stung by taunts from the elders in their city, the Corinthians made their preparations and about twelve days later came back and set up their own trophy as victors. Sallying out from Megara, the Athenians cut off the party that was setting up the trophy, then engaged and defeated the rest. As the beaten army retreated, a sizeable detachment, hard pressed by the pursuers and missing the road, blundered into a piece of private land surrounded by a deep trench, with no way out. Knowing the place, the Athenians sealed off the front with heavy infantry, posted light troops in a ring around it, and stoned to death every man inside. This was a heavy blow to Corinth. The bulk of her army continued its retreat home.

Stroebus’s son, who commanded the landing and the siege after the great sea-battle off Aegina.

Sixty-two days after Tanagra he beat the Boeotians at Oenophyta.

## modern-en · nicomedes · 4:12

About this time the Athenians began to build the long walls down to the sea, one toward Phalerum and one toward Piraeus. Meanwhile the Phocians made an expedition against Doris, the original homeland of the Lacedaemonians, which contained the towns of Boeum, Kitinium, and Erineum. They had taken one of these towns when the Lacedaemonians, under Nicomedes, son of Cleombrotus, who was acting for King Pleistoanax, son of Pausanias, still a minor, came to the Dorians’ aid with fifteen hundred heavy infantry of their own and ten thousand of their allies. After forcing the Phocians to give back the town on terms, they began their retreat. The route by sea, across the Crissaean Gulf, exposed them to being cut off by the Athenian fleet; the route across Geraneia hardly seemed safe either, since the Athenians held Megara and Pegae. The pass was difficult and was always guarded by the Athenians; and on this occasion the Lacedaemonians had information that the Athenians meant to dispute their passage. So they decided to remain in Boeotia and consider which route was safest. They had another reason for staying as well: a party in Athens had quietly encouraged them, hoping to put an end to the democracy and the building of the Long Walls. Meanwhile the Athenians marched against them with their full levy, a thousand Argives, and contingents from the rest of their allies. Altogether they were fourteen thousand strong. The march was prompted by the impression that the Lacedaemonians were at a loss how to get through, and also by suspicion of an attempt to overthrow the democracy. Some cavalry from their Thessalian allies also joined the Athenians, but these went over to the Lacedaemonians during the battle.

Cleombrotus’s son, acting for the boy king Pleistoanax, who brought fifteen hundred Lacedaemonians and ten thousand allies to the help of Doris and then had to sit in Boeotia deciding which way home — encouraged by a party in Athens that wanted the democracy and the Long Walls stopped.

## modern-en · tolmides · 4:13

The battle was fought at Tanagra in Boeotia. After heavy losses on both sides, victory went to the Lacedaemonians and their allies. After moving into the Megarid and cutting down the fruit trees, the Lacedaemonians went home across Geraneia and the isthmus. Sixty-two days after the battle, the Athenians marched into Boeotia under the command of Myronides, defeated the Boeotians at Oenophyta, and became masters of Boeotia and Phocis. They dismantled the walls of Tanagra, took a hundred of the richest men of the Opuntian Locrians as hostages, and finished their own long walls. After this came the surrender of the Aeginetans to Athens on terms: they tore down their walls, gave up their ships, and agreed to pay tribute in future. The Athenians sailed round the Peloponnese under Tolmides, son of Tolmaeus, burnt the Lacedaemonian dockyard, captured Chalcis, a Corinthian town, and in a landing at Sicyon defeated the Sicyonians in battle.

Tolmaeus’s son, who sailed round the Peloponnese, burnt the Lacedaemonian dockyard, took Chalcis and beat the Sicyonians — and who was caught at Coronea on the way home from Chaeronea by the Boeotian exiles, and lost Boeotia with the battle.

## modern-en · orestes · 4:15

Meanwhile Orestes, son of Echecratidas, the Thessalian king, an exile from Thessaly, persuaded the Athenians to restore him. Taking with them the Boeotians and Phocians, their allies, the Athenians marched on Pharsalus in Thessaly. They became masters of the country, but only in the immediate area of the camp; beyond that they could not go for fear of the Thessalian cavalry. They failed to take the city or to achieve any of the other aims of the expedition, and went home with Orestes, having accomplished nothing. Not long after this, a thousand Athenians embarked in the ships at Pegae — Pegae, remember, was now theirs — and sailed along the coast to Sicyon under the command of Pericles, son of Xanthippus. Landing at Sicyon and defeating the Sicyonians who came out to meet them, they immediately picked up the Achaeans, sailed across, and marched against and laid siege to Oeniadae in Acarnania. Failing to take it, they returned home.

Echecratidas’s son, the Thessalian king in exile who persuaded Athens to restore him — and went home with them again, the expedition having accomplished nothing because of the Thessalian cavalry.

Landing at Sicyon and defeating the Sicyonians who came out to meet them, they immediately picked up the Achaeans, sailed across, and marched against and laid siege to Oeniadae in Acarnania. Failing to take it, they returned home.

Meanwhile Orestes, son of Echecratidas, the Thessalian king, an exile from Thessaly, persuaded the Athenians to restore him. They became masters of the country, but only in the immediate area of the camp; beyond that they could not go for fear of the Thessalian cavalry. ## modern-en · pissuthnes · 4:19

Not long after their return from Euboea, they made a thirty-year truce with the Lacedaemonians and their allies, giving up the posts they held in the Peloponnese — Nisaea, Pegae, Troezen, and Achaia. In the sixth year of the truce, war broke out between the Samians and the Milesians over Priene. Beaten in the war, the Milesians came to Athens with loud complaints against the Samians. They were joined by certain private individuals from Samos itself, who wanted to overturn the government. So the Athenians sailed to Samos with forty ships, set up a democracy there, took fifty boys and as many men from the Samians as hostages, lodged them on Lemnos, left a garrison on the island, and went home. But some of the Samians had not stayed on the island; they had fled to the mainland. Coming to an arrangement with the most powerful of those left in the city, and forming an alliance with Pissuthnes, son of Hystaspes, then satrap of Sardis, they got together a force of seven hundred mercenaries and crossed over to Samos under cover of night. Their first move was to rise against the commons, most of whom they secured; their next was to steal back their hostages from Lemnos. Then they openly revolted, handed the Athenian garrison and its commanders over to Pissuthnes, and immediately prepared an expedition against Miletus. The Byzantines also revolted with them.

Hystaspes’s son, satrap of Sardis, who took the Athenian garrison and its commanders off the Samian rebels.

## modern-en · stesagoras · 4:20

As soon as the Athenians heard the news, they sailed against Samos with sixty ships. Sixteen of these were sent off to Caria to watch for the Phoenician fleet, and to Chios and Lesbos to call up reinforcements, and so never engaged. The remaining forty-four, under Pericles and nine colleagues, gave battle off the island of Tragia to seventy Samian ships — twenty of them transports — that were sailing from Miletus. Victory went to the Athenians. Reinforced afterwards by forty ships from Athens and twenty-five from Chios and Lesbos, the Athenians landed, gained the upper hand on land, and surrounded the city with three walls. It was blockaded from the sea as well. Meanwhile Pericles took sixty ships from the blockading squadron and hurried off to Caunus and Caria, on news that the Phoenician fleet was coming to help the Samians; in fact Stesagoras and others had left the island with five ships to bring it. In the meantime the Samians made a sudden sally, fell on the camp, which they found unfortified, destroyed the lookout vessels, and engaged and defeated those that were being launched to meet them. They held command of their own waters for fourteen days and brought in and out whatever they pleased. But on Pericles’ return, they were shut up again. Fresh reinforcements then arrived: forty ships from Athens under Thucydides, Hagnon, and Phormio; twenty under Tlepolemus and Anticles; and thirty from Chios and Lesbos. After a brief attempt at fighting, the Samians, unable to hold out, surrendered after a nine-month siege on terms — they tore down their walls, gave hostages, surrendered their ships, and agreed to pay the cost of the war by instalments. The Byzantines also agreed to be subjects again, as before.

Who left Samos with five ships to bring up the Phoenician fleet — which is why Pericles was off Caria when the Samians broke out and held their own waters for fourteen days.

## modern-en · cylon · 5:7

This interval was spent sending embassies to Athens with complaints, in order to obtain as good a pretext for war as possible if she paid no attention to them. The first Lacedaemonian embassy ordered the Athenians to drive out the curse of the goddess. Its history is as follows. In former generations there was an Athenian named Cylon, an Olympic victor, of good birth and powerful position, who had married a daughter of Theagenes, a Megarian, then tyrant of Megara. This Cylon was inquiring at Delphi when the god told him to seize the Acropolis of Athens at the grand festival of Zeus. Accordingly, getting a force from Theagenes and persuading his friends to join him, when the Olympic festival in the Peloponnese came around, he seized the Acropolis with the intention of making himself tyrant. He thought this was the grand festival of Zeus and an occasion fitting for an Olympic victor. Whether the festival meant was in Attica or elsewhere was a question he never thought about, and the oracle did not offer to settle it. The Athenians too have a festival called the grand festival of Zeus Meilichios, the Gracious—the Diasia. It is celebrated outside the city, where the whole people sacrifice not real victims but a number of bloodless offerings peculiar to the country. Convinced he had picked the right time, he made the attempt. As soon as the Athenians realised what was happening, they flocked in from the country, every man of them, and sat down to besiege the citadel. As time wore on, weary of the labour of blockade, most of them went home, leaving responsibility for the watch to the nine archons, with full powers to settle everything as they saw fit. It must be remembered that at that time most political functions were carried out by the nine archons. Meanwhile Cylon and his besieged companions were in distress for want of food and water. Cylon and his brother managed to escape; but the rest, hard pressed and some even dying of hunger, sat as suppliants at the altar in the Acropolis. The Athenians on guard duty, when they saw them at the point of death in the temple, raised them up on the understanding that no harm would be done to them, led them out, and killed them. Some who took refuge as they passed by at the altars of the awful goddesses were dispatched on the spot. From this deed the men who killed them were called accursed and guilty against the goddess—they and their descendants. These cursed ones were driven out by the Athenians, then driven out again by Cleomenes of Lacedaemon and an Athenian faction; the living were expelled and the bones of the dead taken up; thus they were cast out. Even so they came back later, and their descendants are still in the city.

An Olympic victor of good birth and powerful position, married to a daughter of Theagenes of Megara, who asked at Delphi and was told to seize the Acropolis at the grand festival of Zeus. He took it during the Olympic festival in the Peloponnese, never asking which festival the god meant and never being told — the Athenians have a grand festival of Zeus Meilichios of their own, the Diasia. He and his brother got away; the rest starved at the altar, were raised on a promise of no harm, and were killed. The men who killed them were the accursed, they and their descendants, and driving them out was the first thing Sparta demanded of Athens before the war.

## modern-en · gongylus · 5:9

The Athenians retorted by ordering the Lacedaemonians to drive out the curse of Taenarus. The Lacedaemonians had once raised some Helot suppliants from the temple of Poseidon at Taenarus, led them away, and killed them; for which they believe the great earthquake at Sparta was retribution. The Athenians also ordered them to drive out the curse of the goddess of the Brazen House, the history of which is as follows. After Pausanias the Lacedaemonian had been recalled by the Spartans from his command in the Hellespont (this was his first recall), and had been tried by them and acquitted, he was not sent out again in any official capacity. He took a galley of Hermione on his own responsibility, without Lacedaemonian authority, and arrived as a private person in the Hellespont. He came ostensibly for the Hellenic war, but really to pursue his intrigues with the King, which he had begun before his recall, ambitious to rule over Hellas. The circumstance that first allowed him to put the King in his debt and to begin the whole design was this. Some of the King’s connections and kinsmen had been captured at Byzantium when the city was taken from the Medes, when Pausanias was first there after the return from Cyprus. He sent these prisoners off to the King without the knowledge of the rest of the allies, with the cover story that they had escaped. He arranged this with the help of Gongylus, an Eretrian, whom he had put in charge of Byzantium and the prisoners. He also gave Gongylus a letter for the King, the contents of which—as was later discovered—were as follows: “Pausanias, the general of Sparta, eager to do you a favour, sends you these prisoners of war. I propose also, with your approval, to marry your daughter and to make Sparta and the rest of Hellas subject to you. I believe I am able to do this, with your cooperation. So if any of this pleases you, send a trusty man to the sea through whom we may carry on our future correspondence.”

The Eretrian whom Pausanias put in charge of Byzantium and the captured kinsmen of the King, and who carried the letter offering to make Sparta and Hellas subject to Persia.

## modern-en · artabazus · 5:10

That was all that was revealed in the writing, and Xerxes was pleased with the letter. He sent off Artabazus, son of Pharnaces, to the sea with orders to replace Megabates, the previous governor of the satrapy of Daskylion, and to send across as quickly as possible a letter—entrusted to him—for Pausanias at Byzantium; to show him the royal seal; and to carry out any commission Pausanias might give him on the King’s business with all care and fidelity. Artabazus, on arriving, carried out the King’s orders and sent over the letter, which contained this answer: “Thus says King Xerxes to Pausanias. For the men whom you have saved for me from across the sea from Byzantium, an obligation is laid up for you in our house, recorded forever; and with your proposals I am well pleased. Let neither night nor day stop you from diligently performing any of your promises to me. Let neither cost in gold nor in silver hold them back, nor any number of troops, wherever their presence is needed; but with Artabazus, an honourable man whom I send you, boldly advance my objects and yours, in whatever way most serves the honour and interest of us both.”

Pharnaces’s son, sent down to the sea to replace Megabates in the satrapy of Daskylion, to show Pausanias the royal seal, and to carry the King’s business with him with all care and fidelity.

## modern-en · pleistarchus · 5:13

The Spartans had no tangible proof against him—neither his enemies nor the nation at large had any of that indisputable kind required for the punishment of a member of the royal family who was at that moment in high office. He was regent for his first cousin King Pleistarchus, son of Leonidas, who was still a minor. But by his contempt for the laws and his imitation of the barbarians, he gave grounds for much suspicion that he was discontented with the established order. All the occasions on which he had departed in any way from the regular customs were reviewed, and it was remembered that he had taken it upon himself to have inscribed on the tripod at Delphi—dedicated by the Hellenes as the first-fruits of the spoils of the Medes—the following couplet:

Leonidas’s son, the boy king Pausanias was regent for, and his first cousin.

## modern-en · admetus · 5:18

To return to the Medism of Pausanias. Material was found in the course of the inquiry that implicated Themistocles. The Lacedaemonians accordingly sent envoys to the Athenians, demanding that he be punished as Pausanias had been. The Athenians agreed. As it happened, however, he had been ostracised, and, while living at Argos, was in the habit of visiting other parts of the Peloponnese. So the Athenians sent agents along with the Lacedaemonians, who were ready to join the pursuit, with instructions to take him wherever they found him. But Themistocles got wind of their intentions and fled from the Peloponnese to Corcyra, which was under obligations to him. The Corcyraeans claimed they could not risk sheltering him at the cost of offending Athens and Lacedaemon, and they conveyed him over to the continent opposite. Pursued by officers who hung on every report of his movements, at a loss where to turn, he was forced to stop at the house of Admetus, the Molossian king, though they were not on friendly terms. Admetus happened not to be at home, but his wife, to whom he made himself a suppliant, instructed him to take their child in his arms and sit down by the hearth. Soon afterwards Admetus came in, and Themistocles told him who he was and begged him not to take revenge on a Themistocles in exile for any opposition his requests might have met from Themistocles at Athens. He was now far too low for revenge; retaliation was honourable only between equals. Besides, his opposition to the king had affected only the success of a request, not the safety of his person; if the king were to hand him over to the pursuers he had mentioned, and to the fate they intended for him, he would simply be consigning him to certain death.

The Molossian king, not on friendly terms with Themistocles, who was out when Themistocles arrived; his wife told the suppliant to take their child in his arms and sit by the hearth. He raised him up with the boy still in his arms, refused the Lacedaemonians whatever they said, and sent him overland to the other sea.

## modern-en · ramphias · 5:22

To return to the Lacedaemonians. The history of their first embassy, the demands it conveyed, and the reply it provoked, concerning the expulsion of the accursed persons, has already been told. It was followed by a second embassy, which ordered Athens to raise the siege of Potidæa and to respect the independence of Aegina. Above all, it gave her clearly to understand that war could be averted by revoking the Megara decree, which excluded the Megarians from Athenian harbours and from the market of Athens. But Athens was not inclined either to revoke the decree or to consider their other proposals. She accused the Megarians of pushing their cultivation onto the consecrated ground and the unenclosed land on the border, and of harbouring her runaway slaves. At last an embassy arrived with the Lacedaemonian ultimatum. The ambassadors were Ramphias, Melesippus, and Agesander. Not a word was said on any of the old subjects; there was simply this: “Lacedaemon wishes the peace to continue, and there is no reason it should not, if you would leave the Hellenes independent.” On this, the Athenians held an assembly and laid the matter before them. It was resolved to deliberate once for all on all their demands and to give them an answer. Many speakers came forward and supported one side or the other, urging either the necessity of war, or the revocation of the decree and the folly of letting it stand in the way of peace. Among them came forward Pericles, son of Xanthippus, the foremost man of his time at Athens, ablest alike in counsel and in action, and gave the following advice:

First-named of the three ambassadors who brought the Lacedaemonian ultimatum: not a word on any of the old subjects, only that Lacedaemon wished the peace to continue and there was no reason it should not, if Athens would leave the Hellenes independent.
