## modern-en · zeus · 4:9

Meanwhile the rebels at Ithome, unable to keep up a ten years’ resistance any longer, surrendered to Lacedaemon. The terms were that they should leave the Peloponnese under safe conduct and never set foot in it again: anyone caught there afterwards was to become the slave of his captor. It should be noted that the Lacedaemonians had an old oracle from Delphi telling them to release the suppliant of Zeus at Ithome. So they went out with their children and their wives and were received by Athens — out of the hatred she now felt for the Lacedaemonians — and settled at Naupactus, which Athens had recently taken from the Ozolian Locrians. The Athenians gained another addition to their confederacy in the Megarians, who left the Lacedaemonian alliance, irritated by a war over boundaries that Corinth had forced on them. The Athenians occupied Megara and Pegae, and built the Megarians their long walls from the city to Nisaea, in which they placed an Athenian garrison. This was the main reason the Corinthians conceived such a deadly hatred of Athens.

Whose grand festival Cylon was told to wait for and did not identify — the Athenians have one of their own, to Zeus Meilichios the Gracious, kept outside the city at the Diasia with bloodless offerings. It is his suppliant at Ithome that an old oracle told Lacedaemon to release.

## modern-en · hera · 2:0

The city of Epidamnus stands on the right at the entrance to the Ionic Gulf. The country around it is inhabited by the Taulantians, an Illyrian people. The city is a colony of Corcyra, founded by Phalius, son of Eratocleides, of the Heraclid family. As ancient custom required, he had been summoned for the purpose from Corinth, the mother country. The colonists were joined by some Corinthians and other Dorians. As time went on, Epidamnus grew large and populous; but it fell prey to internal factions, said to have arisen from a war with its barbarian neighbours, and was greatly weakened, losing much of its power. The final act before the war was the people’s expulsion of the nobles. The exiles joined the barbarians and began plundering the city by sea and land. Hard-pressed, the Epidamnians sent ambassadors to Corcyra, begging the mother country not to let them perish, but to settle matters between them and the exiles, and to free them from the war with the barbarians. The ambassadors took their seats as suppliants in the temple of Hera and made these requests of the Corcyraeans. But the Corcyraeans refused their supplication and dismissed them empty-handed.

In whose temple at Corcyra the Epidamnian ambassadors sat as suppliants and were dismissed empty-handed.

## modern-en · apollo · 1:13

But as the power of Hellas grew, and the pursuit of wealth became more pressing, the revenues of the states increased, and tyrannies were established almost everywhere on the strength of those revenues — the older form of government having been hereditary monarchy with defined prerogatives. Hellas began to fit out fleets and apply herself more closely to the sea. The Corinthians are said to have been the first to approach the modern style of naval architecture, and Corinth the first place in Hellas where galleys were built. We have Ameinocles, a Corinthian shipwright, building four ships for the Samians. Counting from the end of this present war, it is nearly three hundred years since Ameinocles went to Samos. Likewise, the earliest sea-fight in history was between the Corinthians and Corcyraeans, about two hundred and sixty years ago, dating from the same point. Set on an isthmus, Corinth had from time immemorial been a commercial hub. In the old days nearly all communication between the Hellenes inside and outside the Peloponnese was carried on overland, and Corinthian territory was the highway it travelled. She therefore had great financial resources — as the epithet “wealthy” given to her by the old poets shows — and this enabled her, when sea trade became more common, to acquire her navy and put down piracy. Since she could provide a market for both branches of the trade, she gained for herself all the power that comes from a large revenue. After this, the Ionians built up great naval strength under Cyrus, the first king of the Persians, and his son Cambyses; and during their war with Cyrus, they commanded for a time the Ionian sea. Polycrates also, the tyrant of Samos, had a powerful navy in the reign of Cambyses, with which he reduced many of the islands, including Rhenea, which he consecrated to the Delian Apollo. Around the same time the Phocaeans, while founding Marseilles, defeated the Carthaginians in a sea-fight. These were the most powerful navies. And even these, although so many generations had passed since the Trojan War, seem to have been made up mainly of the old fifty-oars and long-boats, with few galleys among them. It was only shortly before the Persian War, and the death of Darius the successor of Cambyses, that the Sicilian tyrants and the Corcyraeans acquired any large number of galleys. After these, there were no navies of any consequence in Hellas until the expedition of Xerxes. Aegina, Athens, and others may have had a few vessels, but they were mostly fifty-oars. It was right at the end of this period that the war with Aegina and the prospect of the barbarian invasion enabled Themistocles to persuade the Athenians to build the fleet they fought with at Salamis — and even those vessels did not yet have full decks.

The god at Delphi, who told Epidamnus to hand the city to Corinth, told Cylon to take the Acropolis at the grand festival of Zeus without saying which, told Lacedaemon that if they put their whole strength into the war victory would be theirs and that he himself would be with them invoked or not, and ordered Pausanias’s tomb moved to the place of his death. His temple stands at Actium, and Polycrates gave him Rhenea.

## modern-en · poseidon · 5:9

The Athenians retorted by ordering the Lacedaemonians to drive out the curse of Taenarus. The Lacedaemonians had once raised some Helot suppliants from the temple of Poseidon at Taenarus, led them away, and killed them; for which they believe the great earthquake at Sparta was retribution. The Athenians also ordered them to drive out the curse of the goddess of the Brazen House, the history of which is as follows. After Pausanias the Lacedaemonian had been recalled by the Spartans from his command in the Hellespont (this was his first recall), and had been tried by them and acquitted, he was not sent out again in any official capacity. He took a galley of Hermione on his own responsibility, without Lacedaemonian authority, and arrived as a private person in the Hellespont. He came ostensibly for the Hellenic war, but really to pursue his intrigues with the King, which he had begun before his recall, ambitious to rule over Hellas. The circumstance that first allowed him to put the King in his debt and to begin the whole design was this. Some of the King’s connections and kinsmen had been captured at Byzantium when the city was taken from the Medes, when Pausanias was first there after the return from Cyprus. He sent these prisoners off to the King without the knowledge of the rest of the allies, with the cover story that they had escaped. He arranged this with the help of Gongylus, an Eretrian, whom he had put in charge of Byzantium and the prisoners. He also gave Gongylus a letter for the King, the contents of which—as was later discovered—were as follows: “Pausanias, the general of Sparta, eager to do you a favour, sends you these prisoners of war. I propose also, with your approval, to marry your daughter and to make Sparta and the rest of Hellas subject to you. I believe I am able to do this, with your cooperation. So if any of this pleases you, send a trusty man to the sea through whom we may carry on our future correspondence.”

From whose temple at Taenarus the Lacedaemonians raised the Helot suppliants and led them away and killed them — for which they believe the great earthquake at Sparta was retribution, and which the Athenians told them to drive out as a curse of their own.

## modern-en · athenians · 1:0

Thucydides of Athens wrote the history of the war between the Peloponnesians and the Athenians, beginning the moment it broke out. He believed it would be a great war, more worth recording than any before it. He had grounds for that belief. Both sides were prepared in every department to the highest pitch, and he could see the rest of the Hellenic world taking sides in the quarrel — those who hesitated were already considering it. This was in fact the greatest movement ever known in history, not only among the Hellenes but across much of the barbarian world too — almost, one could say, across all of mankind. The events of remote antiquity, and even those just before the war, could not be clearly established because so much time had passed. But the evidence I have been able to gather, by inquiry pushed back as far as is practicable, all points to the same conclusion: nothing on a great scale had happened before, either in war or in any other sphere.

Whose growth in power, and the alarm it inspired in Lacedaemon, Thucydides gives as the real cause of the war — the cause most kept out of sight in formal speech. Never displaced from Attica because the soil was poor, they laid aside their arms first, took to the sea after the Mede, and turned the confederacy into an empire.

## modern-en · lacedaemonians · 1:5

And even today many parts of Hellas still follow the old way — the Ozolian Locrians, for example, the Aetolians, the Acarnanians, and that whole stretch of mainland. The custom of carrying weapons is still kept up among these mainlanders, a holdover from the old piratical habits. The whole of Hellas once carried arms, since their dwellings were unprotected and their dealings with each other unsafe. To wear arms was as much a part of daily life for them as for the barbarians. The fact that people in these parts of Hellas still live this way points to a time when the same way of life was once common to all. The Athenians were the first to lay aside their weapons and to adopt an easier and more luxurious manner of life. It is only recently that their wealthy older men gave up the luxury of wearing linen undergarments and fastening their hair into a knot held by golden grasshoppers — a fashion that spread to their Ionian kin and long prevailed among the older men there. By contrast, a more modest style of dress, closer to modern taste, was first adopted by the Lacedaemonians, with their wealthy doing their best to assimilate their way of life to that of the common people. They also set the example of competing naked, publicly stripping and oiling themselves for their athletic exercises. In earlier times, even at the Olympic Games, the athletes wore belts around their waists; the practice has only ceased within the last few years. To this day among some of the barbarians, especially in Asia, when prizes are offered for boxing and wrestling, belts are worn. There are many other points on which one could show the likeness between the old Hellenic world and the barbarian world of today.

Slow to go to war except under necessity, with the same form of government for more than four hundred years and no tyrants; they put down the tyrannies of the rest of Hellas, led the alliance against the Mede, and exacted no tribute from their allies, only oligarchies. The Corinthians tell them to their faces that they are the only Hellenes who are inactive, and who defend themselves not by doing anything but by looking as though they might.

## modern-en · potidaeans · 2:28

Almost immediately after this, fresh disputes arose between the Athenians and the Peloponnesians and contributed their share to the war. Corinth was making schemes for revenge, and Athens suspected her hostility. The Potidæans, who inhabit the isthmus of Pallene, being a Corinthian colony but tributary allies of Athens, were ordered to raze the wall facing Pallene, give hostages, dismiss the Corinthian magistrates, and refuse in future to receive the men sent annually from Corinth to succeed them. It was feared that they might be persuaded by Perdiccas and the Corinthians to revolt, and might draw the rest of the allies in the direction of Thrace into revolt with them. These precautions against the Potidæans were taken by the Athenians immediately after the battle at Corcyra. Not only was Corinth at last openly hostile, but Perdiccas, son of Alexander, king of the Macedonians, had been turned from an old friend and ally into an enemy. He had become an enemy because the Athenians had entered into alliance with his brother Philip and with Derdas, who were leagued against him. In his alarm he had sent to Lacedaemon to try to involve the Athenians in war with the Peloponnesians, and was trying to win over Corinth in order to bring about the revolt of Potidæa. He also made overtures to the Chalcidians toward Thrace, and to the Bottiaeans, urging them to join in the revolt, for he thought that if these places on the border could be made his allies, the war would be easier to wage with their cooperation. Aware of all this and wanting to forestall the revolt of the cities, the Athenians acted as follows. They were just then sending off thirty ships and a thousand heavy infantry for his country under the command of Archestratus, son of Lycomedes, with four colleagues. They instructed the captains to take hostages from the Potidæans, raze the wall, and be on guard against the revolt of the neighbouring cities.

A Corinthian colony on the isthmus of Pallene and tributary allies of Athens, ordered to raze the wall facing Pallene, give hostages and dismiss the Corinthian magistrates — and who revolted instead, with the Chalcidians and Bottiaeans, on a Lacedaemonian promise to invade Attica.

## modern-en · illyrians · 2:2

All these grievances made Corinth eager to send the promised aid to Epidamnus. A call went out for volunteer settlers, and a force of Ambraciots, Leucadians, and Corinthians was dispatched. They marched by land to Apollonia, a Corinthian colony, avoiding the sea route for fear of Corcyraean interference. When the Corcyraeans heard that settlers and troops had reached Epidamnus and that the colony had been surrendered to Corinth, they took fire. They put to sea at once with twenty-five ships, soon followed by more, and insolently ordered the Epidamnians to take back the banished nobles—(it must be noted that the Epidamnian exiles had gone to Corcyra and, pointing to the tombs of their ancestors, had appealed to their kinsmen to restore them)—and to dismiss the Corinthian garrison and settlers. But the Epidamnians turned a deaf ear to all of this. The Corcyraeans then began operations against them with a fleet of forty sail. They brought the exiles along with a view to restoring them, and also enlisted the help of the Illyrians. Sitting down before the city, they issued a proclamation: any of the natives who wished, and the foreigners, could leave unharmed; otherwise they would be treated as enemies. When this was refused, the Corcyraeans laid siege to the city, which stands on an isthmus. The Corinthians, hearing that Epidamnus was under blockade, gathered an expedition and proclaimed a colony to Epidamnus, with full political equality guaranteed to all who chose to go. Anyone not ready to sail at once could pay fifty Corinthian drachmae and have a share in the colony without leaving Corinth. Many took up the offer, some ready to sail immediately, others paying the fee. In case the Corcyraeans contested their passage, several cities were asked for a convoy. Megara prepared eight ships, Pale in Cephallonia four, Epidaurus five, Hermione one, Troezen two, Leucas ten, and Ambracia eight. The Thebans and Phliasians were asked for money, the Eleans for hulls as well; Corinth herself furnished thirty ships and three thousand heavy infantry.

Whose help the Corcyraeans enlisted for the siege of Epidamnus.

They marched by land to Apollonia, a Corinthian colony, avoiding the sea route for fear of Corcyraean interference. When the Corcyraeans heard that settlers and troops had reached Epidamnus and that the colony had been surrendered to Corinth, they took fire. They put to sea at once with twenty-five ships, soon followed by more, and insolently ordered the Epidamnians to take back the banished nobles—(it must be noted that the Epidamnian exiles had gone to Corcyra and, pointing to the tombs of their ancestors, had appealed to their kinsmen to restore them)—and to dismiss the Corinthian garrison and settlers. But the Epidamnians turned a deaf ear to all of this. The Corcyraeans then began operations against them with a fleet of forty sail. They brought the exiles along with a view to restoring them, and also enlisted the help of the Illyrians. Sitting down before the city, they issued a proclamation: any of the natives who wished, and the foreigners, could leave unharmed; otherwise they would be treated as enemies. When this was refused, the Corcyraeans laid siege to the city, which stands on an isthmus. The Corinthians, hearing that Epidamnus was under blockade, gathered an expedition and proclaimed a colony to Epidamnus, with full political equality guaranteed to all who chose to go. Anyone not ready to sail at once could pay fifty Corinthian drachmae and have a share in the colony without leaving Corinth. Many took up the offer, some ready to sail immediately, others paying the fee. In case the Corcyraeans contested their passage, several cities were asked for a convoy. Megara prepared eight ships, Pale in Cephallonia four, Epidaurus five, Hermione one, Troezen two, Leucas ten, and Ambracia eight. The Thebans and Phliasians were asked for money, the Eleans for hulls as well; Corinth herself furnished thirty ships and three thousand heavy infantry.

Who sent volunteer settlers and troops to Epidamnus, eight ships for the convoy and twenty-seven for the fleet, and held the right wing with the Megarians at Sybota.

## modern-en · megarians · 2:22

When the Corinthian preparations were complete, they took three days’ provisions and put out from Chimerium by night, ready for action. Sailing at dawn, they sighted the Corcyraean fleet out at sea coming toward them. As soon as the two sides saw each other, they formed in order of battle. On the Corcyraean right wing lay the Athenian ships; the rest of the line was held by Corcyraean vessels formed in three squadrons, each commanded by one of the three admirals. Such was the Corcyraean formation. The Corinthian was as follows: on the right wing lay the Megarian and Ambraciot ships; in the centre, the rest of the allies in order; the left was made up of the best sailers in the Corinthian navy, to face the Athenians and the right wing of the Corcyraeans. As soon as the signals went up on either side, they joined battle. Both sides had a large number of heavy infantry on their decks, and many archers and javelin-throwers, the old, imperfect armament still prevailing. The sea-fight was stubborn but not remarkable for its skill; it was more like a battle on land. Whenever the ships charged, the crowd and crush of vessels made it impossible to disengage easily; besides, both sides relied chiefly on the heavy infantry on the decks, who stood and fought in order while the ships remained stationary. The manoeuvre of breaking the line was not attempted; in short, strength and pluck had more share in the fight than skill. Tumult reigned everywhere, the battle one scene of confusion. Meanwhile the Athenian ships, by coming up to the Corcyraeans whenever they were pressed, alarmed the enemy, though their commanders could not actually join the fight for fear of breaking their orders. The right wing of the Corinthians suffered most. The Corcyraeans routed it, chased its ships in disorder to the continent with twenty of their own vessels, sailed up to the Corinthian camp, burned the empty tents, and plundered the supplies. So in this quarter the Corinthians and their allies were defeated and the Corcyraeans victorious. But where the Corinthians themselves stood, on the left, they had a clear success; the already small Corcyraean force was further weakened by the absence of the twenty ships that had gone in pursuit. Seeing the Corcyraeans hard pressed, the Athenians at last began to assist them more openly. At first they refrained from charging any ships; but as the rout became obvious and the Corinthians pressed on, the moment came when everyone joined in, all distinctions were abandoned, and it came to this: the Corinthians and Athenians raised their hands against each other.

Who left the Lacedaemonian alliance over a boundary war Corinth forced on them, were given long walls and an Athenian garrison, revolted and cut the garrison off — and who came to the congress with a long list of grievances, chief of them exclusion from the ports of the empire and the market of Athens. The decree excluding them was the one thing that could still have averted the war.

## modern-en · samians · 1:13

But as the power of Hellas grew, and the pursuit of wealth became more pressing, the revenues of the states increased, and tyrannies were established almost everywhere on the strength of those revenues — the older form of government having been hereditary monarchy with defined prerogatives. Hellas began to fit out fleets and apply herself more closely to the sea. The Corinthians are said to have been the first to approach the modern style of naval architecture, and Corinth the first place in Hellas where galleys were built. We have Ameinocles, a Corinthian shipwright, building four ships for the Samians. Counting from the end of this present war, it is nearly three hundred years since Ameinocles went to Samos. Likewise, the earliest sea-fight in history was between the Corinthians and Corcyraeans, about two hundred and sixty years ago, dating from the same point. Set on an isthmus, Corinth had from time immemorial been a commercial hub. In the old days nearly all communication between the Hellenes inside and outside the Peloponnese was carried on overland, and Corinthian territory was the highway it travelled. She therefore had great financial resources — as the epithet “wealthy” given to her by the old poets shows — and this enabled her, when sea trade became more common, to acquire her navy and put down piracy. Since she could provide a market for both branches of the trade, she gained for herself all the power that comes from a large revenue. After this, the Ionians built up great naval strength under Cyrus, the first king of the Persians, and his son Cambyses; and during their war with Cyrus, they commanded for a time the Ionian sea. Polycrates also, the tyrant of Samos, had a powerful navy in the reign of Cambyses, with which he reduced many of the islands, including Rhenea, which he consecrated to the Delian Apollo. Around the same time the Phocaeans, while founding Marseilles, defeated the Carthaginians in a sea-fight. These were the most powerful navies. And even these, although so many generations had passed since the Trojan War, seem to have been made up mainly of the old fifty-oars and long-boats, with few galleys among them. It was only shortly before the Persian War, and the death of Darius the successor of Cambyses, that the Sicilian tyrants and the Corcyraeans acquired any large number of galleys. After these, there were no navies of any consequence in Hellas until the expedition of Xerxes. Aegina, Athens, and others may have had a few vessels, but they were mostly fifty-oars. It was right at the end of this period that the war with Aegina and the prospect of the barbarian invasion enabled Themistocles to persuade the Athenians to build the fleet they fought with at Salamis — and even those vessels did not yet have full decks.

For whom Ameinocles built the first four ships, and who went to war with Miletus over Priene, were given a democracy, stole back their hostages from Lemnos, revolted, and held their own waters for fourteen days before a nine-month siege ended it.

They were joined by certain private individuals from Samos itself, who wanted to overturn the government. So the Athenians sailed to Samos with forty ships, set up a democracy there, took fifty boys and as many men from the Samians as hostages, lodged them on Lemnos, left a garrison on the island, and went home. But some of the Samians had not stayed on the island; they had fled to the mainland. Coming to an arrangement with the most powerful of those left in the city, and forming an alliance with Pissuthnes, son of Hystaspes, then satrap of Sardis, they got together a force of seven hundred mercenaries and crossed over to Samos under cover of night. Their first move was to rise against the commons, most of whom they secured; their next was to steal back their hostages from Lemnos. ## modern-en · sicyonians · 4:13

The battle was fought at Tanagra in Boeotia. After heavy losses on both sides, victory went to the Lacedaemonians and their allies. After moving into the Megarid and cutting down the fruit trees, the Lacedaemonians went home across Geraneia and the isthmus. Sixty-two days after the battle, the Athenians marched into Boeotia under the command of Myronides, defeated the Boeotians at Oenophyta, and became masters of Boeotia and Phocis. They dismantled the walls of Tanagra, took a hundred of the richest men of the Opuntian Locrians as hostages, and finished their own long walls. After this came the surrender of the Aeginetans to Athens on terms: they tore down their walls, gave up their ships, and agreed to pay tribute in future. The Athenians sailed round the Peloponnese under Tolmides, son of Tolmaeus, burnt the Lacedaemonian dockyard, captured Chalcis, a Corinthian town, and in a landing at Sicyon defeated the Sicyonians in battle.

Whose envoys went with the Corcyraeans to Corinth, who were beaten by Pericles at Sicyon and by Tolmides before him, and who were let into Megara before the revolt.

## modern-en · chalcidians · 2:28

Almost immediately after this, fresh disputes arose between the Athenians and the Peloponnesians and contributed their share to the war. Corinth was making schemes for revenge, and Athens suspected her hostility. The Potidæans, who inhabit the isthmus of Pallene, being a Corinthian colony but tributary allies of Athens, were ordered to raze the wall facing Pallene, give hostages, dismiss the Corinthian magistrates, and refuse in future to receive the men sent annually from Corinth to succeed them. It was feared that they might be persuaded by Perdiccas and the Corinthians to revolt, and might draw the rest of the allies in the direction of Thrace into revolt with them. These precautions against the Potidæans were taken by the Athenians immediately after the battle at Corcyra. Not only was Corinth at last openly hostile, but Perdiccas, son of Alexander, king of the Macedonians, had been turned from an old friend and ally into an enemy. He had become an enemy because the Athenians had entered into alliance with his brother Philip and with Derdas, who were leagued against him. In his alarm he had sent to Lacedaemon to try to involve the Athenians in war with the Peloponnesians, and was trying to win over Corinth in order to bring about the revolt of Potidæa. He also made overtures to the Chalcidians toward Thrace, and to the Bottiaeans, urging them to join in the revolt, for he thought that if these places on the border could be made his allies, the war would be easier to wage with their cooperation. Aware of all this and wanting to forestall the revolt of the cities, the Athenians acted as follows. They were just then sending off thirty ships and a thousand heavy infantry for his country under the command of Archestratus, son of Lycomedes, with four colleagues. They instructed the captains to take hostages from the Potidæans, raze the wall, and be on guard against the revolt of the neighbouring cities.

Of the Thracian seaboard, who joined the Potidaean revolt and, at Perdiccas’s urging, demolished their own towns and moved inland to Olynthus.

## modern-en · thracians · 4:7

Next come the actions by land and sea at the river Eurymedon between the Athenians and their allies and the Medes, when the Athenians won both battles on the same day under the command of Cimon, son of Miltiades, and captured and destroyed the entire Phoenician fleet of two hundred ships. Some time later came the defection of the Thasians, caused by disagreements about the trading posts on the opposite coast of Thrace and about the mine the Thasians held. Sailing against Thasos, the Athenians defeated them at sea and made a landing on the island. Around the same time they sent out ten thousand settlers — their own citizens and allies — to colonize the place then called Ennea Hodoi, or Nine Ways, now Amphipolis. They managed to take Ennea Hodoi from the Edonians, but on advancing into the interior of Thrace they were cut off at Drabescus, a town of the Edonians, by the assembled Thracians, who saw the settlement at Ennea Hodoi as an act of war. Meanwhile the Thasians, beaten in the field and besieged, appealed to Lacedaemon, asking her to help by invading Attica. Without informing Athens, Sparta promised to do so and intended to, but was prevented by the earthquake — which was followed by the secession of the Helots, and of the Thuriats and Aethaeans of the Perioeci, to Ithome. Most of the Helots were descendants of the old Messenians enslaved in the famous war, so all of them came to be called Messenians. So while the Lacedaemonians were tied up in a war against the rebels at Ithome, the Thasians, in the third year of the siege, came to terms with the Athenians by tearing down their walls, surrendering their ships, agreeing to pay the demanded sums at once and tribute thereafter, and giving up their possessions on the mainland together with the mine.

Who cut the ten thousand Athenian settlers off at Drabescus, taking the settlement at Nine Ways for an act of war.

## modern-en · carians · 1:3

Indeed they could not unite even for that expedition until they had grown more familiar with the sea. The first man known to us by tradition as having built a navy is Minos. He made himself master of what is now called the Hellenic Sea and ruled the Cyclades, sending the first colonies into most of them, expelling the Carians and installing his own sons as governors. He also did his best to put down piracy in those waters — a necessary step to secure the revenues for himself.

Great pirates who colonized most of the islands, expelled from the Cyclades by Minos; more than half the graves opened when Athens purified Delos were theirs, known by the weapons and the manner of burial they still use.

## modern-en · phoenicians · 1:7

The islanders, too, were great pirates. They were Carians and Phoenicians, who had colonized most of the islands. The proof came in this war, when the Athenians purified Delos: all the graves on the island were opened, and more than half of those buried there turned out to be Carians, identified by the style of weapons buried with them and by the manner of burial, which is still used by the Carians today. Once Minos had built his navy, sea travel became easier; he colonized most of the islands and so drove the troublemakers out. The coastal populations now began to apply themselves more closely to the pursuit of wealth, and their lives became more settled. Some even began to build walls on the strength of their newly acquired riches. The love of gain made the weaker accept the rule of the stronger, and possession of capital allowed the more powerful to bring smaller towns under their control. It was at a somewhat later stage of this process that they went on the expedition against Troy.

Islanders and pirates with the Carians; afterwards the King’s navy, which reduced the islands for Darius, lost two hundred ships at the Eurymedon, and was always expected and sometimes never came.

## modern-en · pelasgians · 1:2

There is another point too that strongly confirms my view of the weakness of earlier times. Before the Trojan War there is no sign of any common action across Hellas, nor indeed of the universal use of the name. On the contrary, before the time of Hellen, son of Deucalion, no such name existed at all; the country went by the names of its various tribes, and chiefly by that of the Pelasgians. Only when Hellen and his sons grew strong in Phthiotis, and were brought in as allies by other cities, did those cities one by one acquire the name Hellenes through the connection — though even then a long time passed before that name attached itself to all of them. The best proof comes from Homer. Born long after the Trojan War, he nowhere calls them all Hellenes, nor in fact any of them, except for the followers of Achilles from Phthiotis, who were the original Hellenes. In his poems they are called Danaans, Argives, and Achaeans. He does not even use the word barbarian, probably because the Hellenes had not yet been set apart from the rest of the world by a single distinctive name. So it appears that the various Hellenic communities — both those who first acquired the name, city by city, as they came to understand each other, and those who later adopted it as the name of the whole people — were before the Trojan War prevented by their lack of strength and their isolation from one another from achieving any collective action.

The name the country chiefly went by before Hellen, and the stock the Greek language branched off from.

## modern-en · boeotians · 1:11

From this expedition we can infer the character of earlier ones. Mycenæ may have been a small place, and many of the towns of that age may look comparatively insignificant today, but no careful observer would on that account dismiss the estimate given by the poets and by tradition of the size of the force. Suppose Lacedaemon were to be left desolate, with only the temples and the foundations of public buildings standing — as time went on, posterity would be strongly tempted to dismiss her fame as a true measure of her power. Yet they occupy two-fifths of the Peloponnese and lead the whole, to say nothing of their many allies abroad. Still, since the city is neither built in a compact form nor adorned with magnificent temples and public buildings, but is composed of villages in the old style of Hellas, the impression would be one of inadequacy. Whereas if Athens were to suffer the same fate, I suppose any inference from the appearance left behind would make her power seem twice what it really is. We therefore have no right to be sceptical, nor to content ourselves with inspecting a town and ignoring the question of its real power. We may safely conclude that the force in question outdid every earlier one, as it fell short of modern efforts — if we can also accept Homer's testimony here, in which, even allowing for the exaggeration a poet feels licensed to use, we can see that it came nowhere near our own. He puts it at twelve hundred ships, with each Boeotian ship carrying a hundred and twenty men, and each of Philoctetes's ships carrying fifty. By this, I take it, he meant to give the maximum and minimum complements; at any rate, he does not state the size of any others in his catalogue of ships. That all the men were rowers as well as fighters is shown by his account of the ships of Philoctetes, in which all the men at the oars are bowmen. Now it is unlikely that many supernumeraries sailed, except for the kings and senior officers, especially since they had to cross open sea with their war supplies in ships that had no decks but were rigged in the old piratical style. So if we take the average of the largest and smallest ships, the number of men who sailed will look quite small, considering that they represented the whole strength of Hellas. And this was due not so much to a shortage of men as to a shortage of money. The difficulty of provisioning forced the invaders to keep the army down to a size that could live off the country while the war was being fought. Even after the victory they won on landing — and there must have been a victory, or the fortifications of the naval camp could never have been built — there is no sign that they used their full force. On the contrary, they seem to have turned to cultivating the Chersonese and to piracy from lack of supplies. That was what really enabled the Trojans to hold the field against them for ten years: the enemy was always dispersed, so that the Trojans were always a match for the detachment left behind. If the Greeks had brought plenty of supplies with them and had pressed the war without scattering for piracy and farming, they would easily have beaten the Trojans in pitched battle, since they were able to hold their own even with the divided force on duty. In short, if they had stuck to the siege, the capture of Troy would have cost them less time and less trouble. But as lack of money exposed the weakness of earlier expeditions, so for the same reason even this one, more famous than its predecessors, may be judged from what it actually achieved to have fallen short of its renown and of the prevailing opinion of it that has been formed under the influence of the poets.

Driven out of Arne by the Thessalians sixty years after Ilium and settled in the Cadmeis; beaten at Oenophyta and masters again after Coronea. Homer gives their ships a hundred and twenty men each, the maximum in the catalogue.

## modern-en · thessalians · 1:12

Even after the Trojan War, Hellas was still preoccupied with displacements and resettlements, and so could not arrive at the calm that growth requires. The Hellenes' late return from Ilium triggered many revolutions, and faction broke out almost everywhere. The citizens driven into exile by these upheavals founded the new cities. Sixty years after the capture of Ilium, the modern Boeotians were driven out of Arne by the Thessalians and settled in present-day Boeotia, the former Cadmeis — though a portion of them was already there before, some of whom had joined the expedition to Ilium. Twenty years later the Dorians and the Heraclids became masters of the Peloponnese. So much had to happen, and many years had to pass, before Hellas could reach a stable peace undisturbed by displacements and begin sending out colonies — as Athens did to Ionia and most of the islands, and the Peloponnesians to most of Italy and Sicily and a few other parts of Hellas. All these places were founded after the war with Troy.

Who drove the Boeotians out of Arne; whose cavalry went over to the Lacedaemonians during the battle at Tanagra; and whose horse kept the Athenians inside their camp at Pharsalus.

## modern-en · heraclids · 1:8

What enabled Agamemnon to raise that force was, in my opinion, more his superiority in strength than the oaths of Tyndareus, which bound the suitors to follow him. The account given by those Peloponnesians who have inherited the most credible tradition runs as follows. Pelops first arrived from Asia among a needy population, bringing vast wealth, and acquired such power that, foreigner though he was, the country was named after him. Fortune saw fit to increase that power further in the hands of his descendants. Eurystheus had been killed in Attica by the Heraclids. Atreus was his mother's brother. To this kinsman — who had left his father after the death of Chrysippus — Eurystheus, when he set out on his expedition, had entrusted Mycenæ and the government. As time passed and Eurystheus did not return, Atreus, at the wish of the Mycenaeans, who feared the Heraclids — and since his power seemed considerable, and he had taken care to court the favour of the people — took up the sceptre of Mycenæ and the rest of Eurystheus's dominions. And so the power of the descendants of Pelops came to surpass that of the descendants of Perseus. To all this Agamemnon succeeded. He also had a fleet far stronger than any of his contemporaries, so that in my opinion it was fear quite as much as goodwill that drew the joint expedition together. The strength of his navy is shown by the fact that his own contingent was the largest, and that the Arcadians' ships were supplied by him — at least if we accept Homer on this. Besides, in his account of the transmission of the sceptre, Homer calls him

Who killed Eurystheus in Attica and, with the Dorians, became masters of the Peloponnese; Phalius the founder of Epidamnus was of the family.

From this expedition we can infer the character of earlier ones. ## modern-en · medes · 1:17

But at last a time came when the tyrants of Athens, and the much older tyrannies of the rest of Hellas — with the exception of those in Sicily — were brought down once and for all by Lacedaemon. For Lacedaemon, though after the settlement of the Dorians, her present inhabitants, suffered from faction for an unparalleled length of time, still very early acquired good laws and enjoyed an unbroken freedom from tyrants. She has had the same form of government for more than four hundred years, counting to the end of the late war, and so was in a position to arrange the affairs of the other states. Not many years after the deposition of the tyrants, the battle of Marathon was fought between the Medes and the Athenians. Ten years later the barbarian came back with his armada to subjugate Hellas. In the face of this great danger, the command of the allied Hellenes was taken up by the Lacedaemonians on the strength of their superior power; while the Athenians, having decided to abandon their city, broke up their homes, threw themselves into their ships, and became a naval people. This coalition, after repulsing the barbarian, soon split into two camps, taking in both the Hellenes who had revolted from the King and those who had aided him in the war. At the head of one stood Athens, at the head of the other Lacedaemon — one the leading naval power, the other the leading military power in Hellas. For a short time the league held together, until the Lacedaemonians and Athenians quarrelled and went to war against each other with their allies — a duel into which all the Hellenes were drawn sooner or later, though some at first remained neutral. So that the whole period from the Median War to the present, with some peaceful intervals, was spent by each power in war, either against its rival or against its own revolted allies — and so afforded constant practice in military matters and the kind of experience that is only learned in the school of danger.

The invader of the two invasions, and the measure Thucydides sets this war against: the Median War was the greatest event of past times and was settled in two sea-battles and two land-battles. The rock the barbarian was wrecked on, the Corinthians tell Sparta, was himself.

## modern-en · persians · 1:13

But as the power of Hellas grew, and the pursuit of wealth became more pressing, the revenues of the states increased, and tyrannies were established almost everywhere on the strength of those revenues — the older form of government having been hereditary monarchy with defined prerogatives. Hellas began to fit out fleets and apply herself more closely to the sea. The Corinthians are said to have been the first to approach the modern style of naval architecture, and Corinth the first place in Hellas where galleys were built. We have Ameinocles, a Corinthian shipwright, building four ships for the Samians. Counting from the end of this present war, it is nearly three hundred years since Ameinocles went to Samos. Likewise, the earliest sea-fight in history was between the Corinthians and Corcyraeans, about two hundred and sixty years ago, dating from the same point. Set on an isthmus, Corinth had from time immemorial been a commercial hub. In the old days nearly all communication between the Hellenes inside and outside the Peloponnese was carried on overland, and Corinthian territory was the highway it travelled. She therefore had great financial resources — as the epithet “wealthy” given to her by the old poets shows — and this enabled her, when sea trade became more common, to acquire her navy and put down piracy. Since she could provide a market for both branches of the trade, she gained for herself all the power that comes from a large revenue. After this, the Ionians built up great naval strength under Cyrus, the first king of the Persians, and his son Cambyses; and during their war with Cyrus, they commanded for a time the Ionian sea. Polycrates also, the tyrant of Samos, had a powerful navy in the reign of Cambyses, with which he reduced many of the islands, including Rhenea, which he consecrated to the Delian Apollo. Around the same time the Phocaeans, while founding Marseilles, defeated the Carthaginians in a sea-fight. These were the most powerful navies. And even these, although so many generations had passed since the Trojan War, seem to have been made up mainly of the old fifty-oars and long-boats, with few galleys among them. It was only shortly before the Persian War, and the death of Darius the successor of Cambyses, that the Sicilian tyrants and the Corcyraeans acquired any large number of galleys. After these, there were no navies of any consequence in Hellas until the expedition of Xerxes. Aegina, Athens, and others may have had a few vessels, but they were mostly fifty-oars. It was right at the end of this period that the war with Aegina and the prospect of the barbarian invasion enabled Themistocles to persuade the Athenians to build the fleet they fought with at Salamis — and even those vessels did not yet have full decks.

Whose grandees’ quarters were the few houses left standing in Athens, and who held the White Castle at Memphis and Sestos and Byzantium until they were taken.

## modern-en · phocians · 4:12

About this time the Athenians began to build the long walls down to the sea, one toward Phalerum and one toward Piraeus. Meanwhile the Phocians made an expedition against Doris, the original homeland of the Lacedaemonians, which contained the towns of Boeum, Kitinium, and Erineum. They had taken one of these towns when the Lacedaemonians, under Nicomedes, son of Cleombrotus, who was acting for King Pleistoanax, son of Pausanias, still a minor, came to the Dorians’ aid with fifteen hundred heavy infantry of their own and ten thousand of their allies. After forcing the Phocians to give back the town on terms, they began their retreat. The route by sea, across the Crissaean Gulf, exposed them to being cut off by the Athenian fleet; the route across Geraneia hardly seemed safe either, since the Athenians held Megara and Pegae. The pass was difficult and was always guarded by the Athenians; and on this occasion the Lacedaemonians had information that the Athenians meant to dispute their passage. So they decided to remain in Boeotia and consider which route was safest. They had another reason for staying as well: a party in Athens had quietly encouraged them, hoping to put an end to the democracy and the building of the Long Walls. Meanwhile the Athenians marched against them with their full levy, a thousand Argives, and contingents from the rest of their allies. Altogether they were fourteen thousand strong. The march was prompted by the impression that the Lacedaemonians were at a loss how to get through, and also by suspicion of an attempt to overthrow the democracy. Some cavalry from their Thessalian allies also joined the Athenians, but these went over to the Lacedaemonians during the battle.

Who made an expedition against Doris, the original homeland of the Lacedaemonians, and were made to give the town back; Athens put the temple at Delphi into their hands.

## modern-en · egyptians · 4:10

Meanwhile Inaros, son of Psammetichus, a Libyan king of the Libyans on the Egyptian border, with his headquarters at Marea, the town above Pharos, caused almost the whole of Egypt to revolt from King Artaxerxes. Putting himself at the head of the revolt, he invited the Athenians to come to his aid. Abandoning a Cyprian expedition on which they happened to be engaged with two hundred ships of their own and their allies, the Athenians arrived in Egypt, sailed up from the sea into the Nile, took control of the river and of two-thirds of Memphis, and then turned to attacking the remaining third, which is called White Castle. Inside it were Persians and Medes who had taken refuge there, and Egyptians who had not joined the revolt.

Most of whom revolted from Artaxerxes under Inaros and were beaten back into subjection — except the marshmen, who are the most warlike of them and whose king in the marshes could not be taken.

## modern-en · helots · 4:7

Next come the actions by land and sea at the river Eurymedon between the Athenians and their allies and the Medes, when the Athenians won both battles on the same day under the command of Cimon, son of Miltiades, and captured and destroyed the entire Phoenician fleet of two hundred ships. Some time later came the defection of the Thasians, caused by disagreements about the trading posts on the opposite coast of Thrace and about the mine the Thasians held. Sailing against Thasos, the Athenians defeated them at sea and made a landing on the island. Around the same time they sent out ten thousand settlers — their own citizens and allies — to colonize the place then called Ennea Hodoi, or Nine Ways, now Amphipolis. They managed to take Ennea Hodoi from the Edonians, but on advancing into the interior of Thrace they were cut off at Drabescus, a town of the Edonians, by the assembled Thracians, who saw the settlement at Ennea Hodoi as an act of war. Meanwhile the Thasians, beaten in the field and besieged, appealed to Lacedaemon, asking her to help by invading Attica. Without informing Athens, Sparta promised to do so and intended to, but was prevented by the earthquake — which was followed by the secession of the Helots, and of the Thuriats and Aethaeans of the Perioeci, to Ithome. Most of the Helots were descendants of the old Messenians enslaved in the famous war, so all of them came to be called Messenians. So while the Lacedaemonians were tied up in a war against the rebels at Ithome, the Thasians, in the third year of the siege, came to terms with the Athenians by tearing down their walls, surrendering their ships, agreeing to pay the demanded sums at once and tribute thereafter, and giving up their possessions on the mainland together with the mine.

Most of them descendants of the old Messenians enslaved in the famous war, which is why they all came to be called Messenians. They seceded to Ithome after the earthquake; Pausanias promised them freedom and citizenship to join him; and the suppliants raised from the temple of Poseidon at Taenarus and killed were Helots.

## modern-en · dolopians · 4:6

First the Athenians besieged and captured Eion on the Strymon from the Medes, and enslaved the inhabitants, under the command of Cimon, son of Miltiades. Next they enslaved Scyros, the island in the Aegean, which had a Dolopian population, and colonized it themselves. Then came a war against Carystus, in which the rest of Euboea remained neutral; it ended in surrender on terms. After this Naxos left the confederacy, war followed, and after a siege she was forced back in. This was the first instance of the agreement being broken by the subjugation of an allied city — a precedent the rest followed in the order circumstances dictated. Of all the causes of defection, the chief was failure to keep up the tribute and the supply of vessels, and failure to perform service. The Athenians were severe and exacting, and they made themselves offensive by applying the screw to men who were not used to and not really inclined to any continuous effort. In other respects too the Athenians were no longer the popular leaders they had been at the start. If they took more than their fair share of the work, it was correspondingly easy for them to bring back into line any state that tried to leave the confederacy. The allies had themselves to blame for this. Most of them, wanting to avoid service, arranged to pay their share of the cost in money instead of in ships, so as not to leave home. So while Athens was building up her navy on the funds they contributed, a revolt would always find them without resources or experience for war.

The population of Scyros, enslaved by Cimon.

## modern-en · cyprians · 4:16

Three years later a truce was made between the Peloponnesians and the Athenians for five years. Released from war within Hellas, the Athenians made an expedition to Cyprus with two hundred ships of their own and their allies, under Cimon’s command. Sixty of these were detached to Egypt at the request of Amyrtaeus, the king in the marshes; the rest laid siege to Kitium, from which, however, they were forced to withdraw because of Cimon’s death and a shortage of provisions. Sailing off Salamis in Cyprus, they fought the Phoenicians, Cyprians, and Cilicians by land and sea, and, victorious on both elements, they returned home together with the squadron that had come back from Egypt. After this the Lacedaemonians launched a sacred war, gained control of the temple at Delphi, and put it in the hands of the Delphians. As soon as they had withdrawn, the Athenians marched out, took the temple, and put it in the hands of the Phocians.

Beaten with the Phoenicians and Cilicians off Salamis in Cyprus.

## modern-en · argives · 1:2

There is another point too that strongly confirms my view of the weakness of earlier times. Before the Trojan War there is no sign of any common action across Hellas, nor indeed of the universal use of the name. On the contrary, before the time of Hellen, son of Deucalion, no such name existed at all; the country went by the names of its various tribes, and chiefly by that of the Pelasgians. Only when Hellen and his sons grew strong in Phthiotis, and were brought in as allies by other cities, did those cities one by one acquire the name Hellenes through the connection — though even then a long time passed before that name attached itself to all of them. The best proof comes from Homer. Born long after the Trojan War, he nowhere calls them all Hellenes, nor in fact any of them, except for the followers of Achilles from Phthiotis, who were the original Hellenes. In his poems they are called Danaans, Argives, and Achaeans. He does not even use the word barbarian, probably because the Hellenes had not yet been set apart from the rest of the world by a single distinctive name. So it appears that the various Hellenic communities — both those who first acquired the name, city by city, as they came to understand each other, and those who later adopted it as the name of the whole people — were before the Trojan War prevented by their lack of strength and their isolation from one another from achieving any collective action.

Sparta’s enemy, whom the Athenians allied with the moment they were sent home from Ithome, and a thousand of whom marched with them to Tanagra. Themistocles in exile lived among them and kept secret hoards there.

## modern-en · histiaeans · 4:18

This was soon followed by the revolt of Euboea from Athens. Pericles had already crossed over to the island with an Athenian army when news reached him that Megara had revolted, that the Peloponnesians were about to invade Attica, and that the Athenian garrison had been cut off by the Megarians, except for a few who had taken refuge in Nisaea. The Megarians had let the Corinthians, Sicyonians, and Epidaurians into the town before they revolted. Meanwhile Pericles brought his army back from Euboea as fast as he could. After this the Peloponnesians invaded Attica as far as Eleusis and Thrius, ravaging the country under the command of King Pleistoanax, son of Pausanias, and went home without advancing further. The Athenians then crossed back to Euboea under Pericles and subdued the whole island. All but Histiaea was settled by agreement; the Histiaeans they expelled from their homes and took the territory for themselves.

Alone of Euboea not settled by agreement: Athens expelled them from their homes and took the territory.

## modern-en · byzantines · 4:19

Not long after their return from Euboea, they made a thirty-year truce with the Lacedaemonians and their allies, giving up the posts they held in the Peloponnese — Nisaea, Pegae, Troezen, and Achaia. In the sixth year of the truce, war broke out between the Samians and the Milesians over Priene. Beaten in the war, the Milesians came to Athens with loud complaints against the Samians. They were joined by certain private individuals from Samos itself, who wanted to overturn the government. So the Athenians sailed to Samos with forty ships, set up a democracy there, took fifty boys and as many men from the Samians as hostages, lodged them on Lemnos, left a garrison on the island, and went home. But some of the Samians had not stayed on the island; they had fled to the mainland. Coming to an arrangement with the most powerful of those left in the city, and forming an alliance with Pissuthnes, son of Hystaspes, then satrap of Sardis, they got together a force of seven hundred mercenaries and crossed over to Samos under cover of night. Their first move was to rise against the commons, most of whom they secured; their next was to steal back their hostages from Lemnos. Then they openly revolted, handed the Athenian garrison and its commanders over to Pissuthnes, and immediately prepared an expedition against Miletus. The Byzantines also revolted with them.

Who revolted with the Samians and agreed to be subjects again, as before.

## modern-en · sermylians · 2:33

Siege-works were now raised and manned by the Athenians on the isthmus side of the wall. On the Pallene side none were raised. They did not feel strong enough at once to keep a garrison on the isthmus and cross over to Pallene to raise works there; they feared that the Potidæans and their allies might take advantage of their division to attack them. Meanwhile the Athenians at home, learning that there were no works on the Pallene side, some time later sent out sixteen hundred heavy infantry of their own citizens under the command of Phormio, son of Asopius. On reaching Pallene he fixed his headquarters at Aphytis and led his army against Potidæa by short marches, ravaging the country as he advanced. Since no one ventured to meet him in the field, he raised works against the wall on the Pallene side. So at last Potidæa was strongly invested on both sides, and from the sea by the ships cooperating in the blockade. Aristeus, seeing the investment complete and having no hope of saving the place except in the event of some movement from the Peloponnese, or some other improbable contingency, advised that all but five hundred men should watch for a wind and sail out of the place, so that provisions might last longer. He was willing to remain himself among those who stayed. Unable to persuade them, and wishing to take the next best course and have things outside in the best possible state, he eluded the Athenian guardships and sailed out. Remaining with the Chalcidians, he carried on the war: in particular he laid an ambush near the city of the Sermylians and cut off many of them; he also communicated with the Peloponnese and tried to contrive some method of bringing help. Meanwhile, after the investment of Potidæa was complete, Phormio used his sixteen hundred men to ravage Chalcidice and Bottica, and took some of the towns.

Many of whom Aristeus cut off in an ambush near their city.

## modern-en · zacynthians · 6:8

The allies of the two belligerents were these. On the Lacedaemonian side: all the Peloponnesians within the Isthmus except the Argives and Achaeans, who were neutral; Pellene was the only Achaean city that joined in the war from the start, though the others followed her lead later. Outside the Peloponnese: the Megarians, Locrians, Boeotians, Phocians, Ambraciots, Leucadians, and Anactorians. Of these, ships were supplied by the Corinthians, Megarians, Sicyonians, Pellenians, Eleans, Ambraciots, and Leucadians; cavalry by the Boeotians, Phocians, and Locrians. The other states sent infantry. That was the Lacedaemonian confederacy. The Athenian confederacy consisted of the Chians, Lesbians, Plataeans, the Messenians at Naupactus, most of the Acarnanians, the Corcyraeans, the Zacynthians, and various tributary cities in the following regions: Caria on the coast with her Dorian neighbours, Ionia, the Hellespont, the Thracian towns, the islands lying between the Peloponnese and Crete to the east, and all the Cyclades except Melos and Thera. Of these, ships were supplied by Chios, Lesbos, and Corcyra; the rest provided infantry and money. Such were the allies of either side, and such their resources for the war.

A thousand of whose heavy infantry came to help the Corcyraeans and were posted on Point Leukimme.

## modern-en · molossians · 5:18

To return to the Medism of Pausanias. Material was found in the course of the inquiry that implicated Themistocles. The Lacedaemonians accordingly sent envoys to the Athenians, demanding that he be punished as Pausanias had been. The Athenians agreed. As it happened, however, he had been ostracised, and, while living at Argos, was in the habit of visiting other parts of the Peloponnese. So the Athenians sent agents along with the Lacedaemonians, who were ready to join the pursuit, with instructions to take him wherever they found him. But Themistocles got wind of their intentions and fled from the Peloponnese to Corcyra, which was under obligations to him. The Corcyraeans claimed they could not risk sheltering him at the cost of offending Athens and Lacedaemon, and they conveyed him over to the continent opposite. Pursued by officers who hung on every report of his movements, at a loss where to turn, he was forced to stop at the house of Admetus, the Molossian king, though they were not on friendly terms. Admetus happened not to be at home, but his wife, to whom he made himself a suppliant, instructed him to take their child in his arms and sit down by the hearth. Soon afterwards Admetus came in, and Themistocles told him who he was and begged him not to take revenge on a Themistocles in exile for any opposition his requests might have met from Themistocles at Athens. He was now far too low for revenge; retaliation was honourable only between equals. Besides, his opposition to the king had affected only the success of a request, not the safety of his person; if the king were to hand him over to the pursuers he had mentioned, and to the fate they intended for him, he would simply be consigning him to certain death.

Admetus’s people, with whom Themistocles took sanctuary.

## modern-en · eretrians · 5:9

The Athenians retorted by ordering the Lacedaemonians to drive out the curse of Taenarus. The Lacedaemonians had once raised some Helot suppliants from the temple of Poseidon at Taenarus, led them away, and killed them; for which they believe the great earthquake at Sparta was retribution. The Athenians also ordered them to drive out the curse of the goddess of the Brazen House, the history of which is as follows. After Pausanias the Lacedaemonian had been recalled by the Spartans from his command in the Hellespont (this was his first recall), and had been tried by them and acquitted, he was not sent out again in any official capacity. He took a galley of Hermione on his own responsibility, without Lacedaemonian authority, and arrived as a private person in the Hellespont. He came ostensibly for the Hellenic war, but really to pursue his intrigues with the King, which he had begun before his recall, ambitious to rule over Hellas. The circumstance that first allowed him to put the King in his debt and to begin the whole design was this. Some of the King’s connections and kinsmen had been captured at Byzantium when the city was taken from the Medes, when Pausanias was first there after the return from Cyprus. He sent these prisoners off to the King without the knowledge of the rest of the allies, with the cover story that they had escaped. He arranged this with the help of Gongylus, an Eretrian, whom he had put in charge of Byzantium and the prisoners. He also gave Gongylus a letter for the King, the contents of which—as was later discovered—were as follows: “Pausanias, the general of Sparta, eager to do you a favour, sends you these prisoners of war. I propose also, with your approval, to marry your daughter and to make Sparta and the rest of Hellas subject to you. I believe I am able to do this, with your cooperation. So if any of this pleases you, send a trusty man to the sea through whom we may carry on our future correspondence.”

Whose old war with Chalcis was the nearest thing to a coalition in Hellas before the Median War; and Gongylus was one of them.

## modern-en · arcadians · 1:8

What enabled Agamemnon to raise that force was, in my opinion, more his superiority in strength than the oaths of Tyndareus, which bound the suitors to follow him. The account given by those Peloponnesians who have inherited the most credible tradition runs as follows. Pelops first arrived from Asia among a needy population, bringing vast wealth, and acquired such power that, foreigner though he was, the country was named after him. Fortune saw fit to increase that power further in the hands of his descendants. Eurystheus had been killed in Attica by the Heraclids. Atreus was his mother's brother. To this kinsman — who had left his father after the death of Chrysippus — Eurystheus, when he set out on his expedition, had entrusted Mycenæ and the government. As time passed and Eurystheus did not return, Atreus, at the wish of the Mycenaeans, who feared the Heraclids — and since his power seemed considerable, and he had taken care to court the favour of the people — took up the sceptre of Mycenæ and the rest of Eurystheus's dominions. And so the power of the descendants of Pelops came to surpass that of the descendants of Perseus. To all this Agamemnon succeeded. He also had a fleet far stronger than any of his contemporaries, so that in my opinion it was fear quite as much as goodwill that drew the joint expedition together. The strength of his navy is shown by the fact that his own contingent was the largest, and that the Arcadians' ships were supplied by him — at least if we accept Homer on this. Besides, in his account of the transmission of the sceptre, Homer calls him

Whose part of the Peloponnese was poor enough to be left alone by the changes of masters, and whose ships at Troy were supplied by Agamemnon.

## modern-en · epidaurians · 4:11

Meanwhile the Athenians, making a landing from their fleet at Haliae, were engaged by a force of Corinthians and Epidaurians, and the Corinthians won. Afterwards the Athenians fought the Peloponnesian fleet off Cecruphalia, and the Athenians won. Then war broke out between Aegina and Athens. There was a great sea battle off Aegina between Athenians and Aeginetans, each side helped by their allies; the Athenians won, taking seventy of the enemy’s ships. They landed and began a siege of the country under the command of Leocrates, son of Stroebus. At this the Peloponnesians, wanting to help the Aeginetans, threw three hundred heavy infantry — who had previously been serving with the Corinthians and Epidaurians — into Aegina. Meanwhile the Corinthians and their allies seized the heights of Geraneia and marched down into the Megarid, in the belief that with so large an Athenian force absent in Aegina and Egypt, Athens would be unable to help the Megarians without lifting the siege of Aegina. But the Athenians, instead of moving the army at Aegina, raised a force from the old men and the boys who had been left in the city, and marched into the Megarid under the command of Myronides. After a drawn battle with the Corinthians, the two sides parted, each thinking they had won. The Athenians had rather the better of it, and after the Corinthians left they set up a trophy. Stung by taunts from the elders in their city, the Corinthians made their preparations and about twelve days later came back and set up their own trophy as victors. Sallying out from Megara, the Athenians cut off the party that was setting up the trophy, then engaged and defeated the rest. As the beaten army retreated, a sizeable detachment, hard pressed by the pursuers and missing the road, blundered into a piece of private land surrounded by a deep trench, with no way out. Knowing the place, the Athenians sealed off the front with heavy infantry, posted light troops in a ring around it, and stoned to death every man inside. This was a heavy blow to Corinth. The bulk of her army continued its retreat home.

Who sent five ships for the Epidamnus convoy, beat the Athenians with the Corinthians at Haliae, and were let into Megara before the revolt.

Their king, Agis, accordingly set out at once during this winter with some troops from Decelea, levied contributions from the allies for the fleet, and turning towards the Malian Gulf exacted a sum of money from the Oetaeans by carrying off most of their cattle in reprisal for their old hostility, and, in spite of the protests and opposition of the Thessalians, forced the Achaeans of Phthiotis and the other subjects of the Thessalians in those parts to give him money and hostages, and deposited the hostages at Corinth, and tried to bring their countrymen into the confederacy. The Lacedaemonians now issued a requisition to the cities for building a hundred ships, fixing their own quota and that of the Boeotians at twenty-five each; that of the Phocians and Locrians together at fifteen; that of the Corinthians at fifteen; that of the Arcadians, Pellenians, and Sicyonians together at ten; and that of the Megarians, Troezenians, Epidaurians, and Hermionians together also at ten; and meanwhile they made every other preparation to begin hostilities by the spring.

Who sent one ship for the convoy; Pausanias took a galley of theirs on his own responsibility for his second voyage out.

## modern-en · chians · 6:8

The allies of the two belligerents were these. On the Lacedaemonian side: all the Peloponnesians within the Isthmus except the Argives and Achaeans, who were neutral; Pellene was the only Achaean city that joined in the war from the start, though the others followed her lead later. Outside the Peloponnese: the Megarians, Locrians, Boeotians, Phocians, Ambraciots, Leucadians, and Anactorians. Of these, ships were supplied by the Corinthians, Megarians, Sicyonians, Pellenians, Eleans, Ambraciots, and Leucadians; cavalry by the Boeotians, Phocians, and Locrians. The other states sent infantry. That was the Lacedaemonian confederacy. The Athenian confederacy consisted of the Chians, Lesbians, Plataeans, the Messenians at Naupactus, most of the Acarnanians, the Corcyraeans, the Zacynthians, and various tributary cities in the following regions: Caria on the coast with her Dorian neighbours, Ionia, the Hellespont, the Thracian towns, the islands lying between the Peloponnese and Crete to the east, and all the Cyclades except Melos and Thera. Of these, ships were supplied by Chios, Lesbos, and Corcyra; the rest provided infantry and money. Such were the allies of either side, and such their resources for the war.

Who with the Lesbians alone were never deprived of their ships and made to pay money instead, and who sent twenty-five ships to the siege of Samos.

## modern-en · phaeacians · 2:1

When the Epidamnians found that no help could be expected from Corcyra, they did not know what to do next. So they sent to Delphi and asked the god whether they should hand over their city to the Corinthians and try to obtain some assistance from their founders. The god answered that they should hand over the city and place themselves under Corinthian protection. The Epidamnians went to Corinth and surrendered the colony in obedience to the oracle. They pointed out that their founder had come from Corinth, repeated the god’s answer, and begged the Corinthians not to let them perish but to assist them. The Corinthians agreed. They believed the colony belonged as much to themselves as to the Corcyraeans, and they felt a kind of duty to take its protection in hand. Besides, they hated the Corcyraeans for the contempt they showed the mother country. Other colonies honoured Corinth at public assemblies in the usual ways—precedence at sacrifices, for instance—but Corcyra treated her with contempt. And Corcyra was wealthy enough to stand comparison with the richest communities in Hellas, militarily strong, and at times openly proud of her naval reputation, which dated back to her old inhabitants, the Phaeacians. This was one reason the Corcyraeans took such care of their fleet, which became very efficient: they began the war with a force of one hundred and twenty galleys.

Corcyra’s old inhabitants, from whom the Corcyraeans date the naval reputation they were at times openly proud of.

## modern-en · tanagraeans · 11:4

The same summer the Athenians sent thirty ships round the Peloponnese under Demosthenes, son of Alcisthenes, and Procles, son of Theodorus, and sixty more, with two thousand heavy infantry, against Melos under Nicias, son of Niceratus. They wanted to reduce the Melians, who, although islanders, refused to be subjects of Athens or even to join her confederacy. The devastation of their land did not bring them to terms, so the fleet weighed from Melos and sailed to Oropus in the territory of Graea. Landing at nightfall, the heavy infantry started at once from the ships by land for Tanagra in Boeotia, where they were met, by prearranged signal, by the whole levy from Athens, under Hipponicus, son of Callias, and Eurymedon, son of Thucles. They encamped, and after spending that day ravaging the territory of Tanagra, stayed there for the night. The next day, after defeating those of the Tanagraeans who came out against them and some Thebans who had come up to help them, they took some arms, set up a trophy, and withdrew — the troops to the city, the rest to the ships. Nicias, with his sixty ships, coasted along the shore, ravaged the Locrian seaboard, and so returned home.

Whose walls the Athenians dismantled after Oenophyta.

## modern-en · argilians · 5:15

At the time the Lacedaemonians had erased the couplet at once and inscribed instead the names of the cities that had aided in the overthrow of the barbarian and dedicated the offering. Pausanias was considered to have been guilty of a grave offence here—one which, interpreted in the light of the attitude he had since taken, gained a new significance and seemed perfectly in keeping with his current schemes. They were also informed that he was even intriguing with the Helots; and so it was, for he promised them freedom and citizenship if they would join him in revolt and help him carry out his plans to the end. Even now, mistrusting the evidence even of the Helots themselves, the ephors would not consent to take any decisive step against him, in line with their regular custom toward fellow Spartans—to be slow to take any irrevocable decision in the case of a Spartan citizen without indisputable proof. At last, it is said, the man who was to carry the latest letter to Artabazus for the King, an Argilian and once Pausanias’s favourite and most trusted servant, turned informer. Alarmed by the thought that none of the previous messengers had ever returned, he had counterfeited the seal so that, if he were mistaken in his suspicions, or if Pausanias asked to make some change, he would not be found out. He undid the letter and found the postscript he had suspected—an order to put him to death.

One of them was Pausanias’s favourite and most trusted servant, and the courier who counterfeited the seal, found the postscript ordering his own death, and turned informer.
